#2957. Can the Sixers keep it rolling against challenging opponents?

For as bad as last Friday's game was in Cleveland, the Sixers probably deserve a mulligan. At 9-2 after Sunday's win in Chicago, Philly has not only looked the part of a championship contender, but they've done it against lots of fellow contenders, Sunday notwithstanding. The schedule is about to lighten up for the Sixers, but they still have two more games this week against playoff teams from last season. They'll host Boston on Tuesday and Toronto on Thursday.

If the Sixers can get to 11-2, they'll be set up to face a slew of sub-.500 teams for the rest of November. They've already got the attention of the entire NBA world given that they've lived up to the preseason hype thus far. Think about what the narrative could be in 3-4 week's time surrounding this team. The wins should continue to be piled up, and they would have already beaten all the top challengers in the Eastern Conference once already and it wouldn't even be Christmas yet.

Oddly enough, the Sixers have not had home-court advantage in a playoff series since the 2023 first round when they swept Brooklyn. That means it hasn't happened in three previous seasons under Nick Nurse. As they continue to stockpile victories early in the season, the reality of a deep playoff run becomes increasingly plausible. In two postseasons under Nurse, the Sixers have had grueling first-round matchups, losing a tight series to New York in 2024 and having to rally from 3-1 down to eliminate Boston in 2026. The drain of having to dig out of the 3-1 hole against Boston clearly showed itself in the second round last spring when the Sixers got whitewashed by the Knicks in a quick four-game sweep.

In 2023, Philly was ahead 3-2 after five games against Boston and on the precipice of making the Eastern Conference Finals for the first time since 2001. We all know how the series ended, but the point remains that getting through an easy first-round series would do wonders for getting Joel Embiid — and LeBron James at his advanced age, for that matter — through an entire four rounds of the postseason.

As the Sixers continue to stack victories early in the 2026-27 season, they'll continue to make the path to an NBA Championship easier for themselves. Not only have they been stacking victories but finishing off such a challenging first three weeks of the season with two more wins this week against Boston and Toronto would only be the icing on a cake that's already baking pretty good. In addition to making their postseason path easier, Philly could be as selective as possible in determining when to load manage Embiid and James.

The NBA regular season has often come with a label of irrelevance thanks in part to the load managing we see around the league with superstars and their playing time. While it feels like the Sixers have washed away a lot of the negative labels surrounding their ability to make a deep playoff run, there probably is a segment of the fanbase that is still very much in "Wake me up in April" mode and, to some extent, that's an understandable sentiment to feel.

But the Sixers are doing all they can possibly do now to make fans believe that this could be the year they play into June. They're beating every contender in front of them. Remember, despite Friday's drubbing in Cleveland, they came back from a 29-point deficit to beat the Cavaliers earlier in the season. Simply put, it's a great time to be a Sixers fan, even if it's only November.

#2953. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2949. Buffalo Bills elevate WR Trent Sherfield Sr., S Geno Stone for Week 9 of ‘MNF’

The Buffalo Bills have announced a series of roster moves in the hours leading up to their Week 9 game in primetime against the Minnesota Vikings. Buffalo has elevated wide receiver Trent Sherfield Sr. and safety Geno Stone.

This is the second consecutive elevation for Stone, who played 19 defensive snaps (37%) in Week 8 against the Baltimore Ravens. Stone made six tackles, which included two solo tackles and one tackle for loss. He also contributed on special teams, logging 16 snaps (59%) against Baltimore.

Sherfield also sees his second elevation this season, having previously been elevated Week 5 for Buffalo's road game against the Los Angeles Rams. He logged seven offensive snaps (11%), but didn't register a reception or yards. Sherfield made a bigger impact on special teams, where he was on the field for 17 snaps (59%), making one solo tackle and one combined tackle.

One Bills Drive also placed wide receiver Joshua Palmer on Injured Reserve (IR), which will sideline him for at least the next four regular-season games. With Palmer heading to IR before the trade deadline, it means he won't be subjected to an in-season trade.

Palmer was injured in action against the Ravens in Week 8. Through six games in 2026, Palmer has managed just four receptions (12 targets) for 117 yards, with two touchdowns and three of his receptions going for first-down yardage.

#2944. Rockets need to reclaim their identity

The Houston Rockets are having an identity crisis.

Think Fight Club (which, as an aside, I only watched for the first time a few years ago, and if you haven't seen it, don't bother; it's been caricatured to the point that it feels like a parody of itself), in reverse. The Rockets were a rugged, fist-wielding Brad Pitt in 2025-26. Pesky social conventions like "getting good shots" and "running offensive sets" were boring and beneath them. The Rockets were winning fights instead of playing games.

In 2026-27, they're more like the neutered, emasculated Edward Norton. They've mostly been doing all the right things, and it's mostly been working, but they're just lacking some kind of cliched, overly white-male-privileged (blue wavy baby, we're back) existential purpose.

Let's park the analogy. It's good that the Rockets' offense has looked more sophisticated in 2026-27. They shouldn't abandon that. Instead, they need to synthesize last season's approach with this one.

In their last contest, the old 2025-26 offense reared its ugly (or maybe ruggedly handsome if we're Brad Pitt) head. The floor shrank. Instead of zipping high post passes to a cutting Amen Thompson, Alperen Sengun settled for a bunch of bad floaters that he's never been proficient with, in large part because the Thompson cuts weren't coming. The Rockets' offense fell apart:

Yet, if they'd just dominated the Suns on the glass, they would have won anyway.

Rest assured, this offense will fall apart again.

Ultimately, it's the same old chestnut: The Rockets don't have an every-night, carry-the-offense, even if he's missing, he's making something happen superstar. Sorry, Sengun fans, and Thompson isn't even close, and it's just not how the team is using Kevin Durant this year, which makes sense from a longer-term developmental perspective and a shorter-term, preserve him for the playoffs perspective.

Last year, the Rockets compensated for that lack of top-end talent by dominating physically. They were number one by a long shot in Offensive Rebounding %. This year, they're fourth (36.0%). They're fifth in overall Rebounding % (52.5th), which they also led the NBA in last season.

Sure, the Rockets are still a great rebounding team. If they have 2026-27 NBA championship aspirations, great isn't great enough. They need to be the best rebounding team in the NBA, or else they will lose in the playoffs to a team with better players.

It's not just rebounding, either. The Rockets' 109.4 Defensive Rating ranks 10th in the NBA. Last year, they had the league's sixth-best defense (112.1). This year's mark is actually better, but that feels like a small-sample quirk. Their placement relative to the league seems more relevant.

Of course, they also have the second-best Offensive Rating in the NBA (118.6). Make no mistake: This year's team looks better than last year's team. It would also be wise to avoid overreacting to one game, but against the Suns, it felt like some offensive regression was due. If it is, they need to regress to the mean (a confusing phrase when something is improving; it ought to be progress to the mean, why don't we say that?) in rebounding and defense.

Some of this is due to Steven Adams' absence, but let's not make excuses. If Adams can't regularly play, the Rockets need a new backup five, and he needs to be the best rebounder they can find. The Rockets need to rediscover their identity:

After all, the things you do own, end up owning you.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2941. Jaguars land DT Jordan Davis in blockbuster deal with Eagles, marking latest trade ahead of deadline

The Jacksonville Jaguars are going all in.

The Jaguars struck a deal to land Philadelphia Eagles defensive tackle Jordan Davis on Monday afternoon, just a day ahead of the NFL trade deadline, the team announced. In return, the Eagles are getting multiple draft picks, including a first-round selection for next April.

The deal is the third the Jaguars made on Monday to add a defensive player. They acquired safety Jessie Bates III in a trade with the Atlanta Falcons, and also picked up defensive tackle Davon Godchaux in a trade with the New Orleans Saints.

Davis is in his fifth season in the league. He racked up a career-high 72 total tackles and 4.5 sacks during the 2025 campaign, and has 31 total tackles and 1.5 sacks through nine games this season. The 26-year-old, whom the Eagles selected with the No. 13 overall pick in 2022 out of Georgia, signed a three-year, $78 million extension with the Eagles in March.

"We are extremely grateful to Jordan for his contributions to our team on the field and in the Philadelphia community since we drafted him five years ago. He has been a key contributor to our team culture and on-field success, which included two Super Bowl appearances and a world championship in 2024," the Eagles said in a statement.

"His growth and development as both a player and a leader are a testament to his hard work and character. We wish Jordan nothing but continued success."

Philadelphia is on a seven-game skid after Sunday's 19-11 loss to the New York Giants. The Eagles (2-7) are last in the NFC East and are all but out of the playoff race this season — especially with quarterback Jalen Hurts sidelined due to blood clots. The latest move gives the Eagles an additional first-round pick in next year's draft and a second-round pick in 2029.

They now have a combined 10 picks in the first two rounds of the next three drafts, including two in the first round in both 2027 and 2028. That should give the organization plenty of room to regroup after this season ends.

Meanwhile, Jacksonville clearly feels like it has a roster capable of making a Super Bowl push, and Monday's moves only bolster that feeling. The three additions will complement an already tough defense that has allowed just 15.1 points per game this season — which is the second-best output in the league behind only the Minnesota Vikings. The Jags sit at 6-2 coming out of Week 9, after they beat the Baltimore Ravens 33-10 for their fifth win in the past six outings.

Jacksonville no longer has a first- or second-round draft pick in 2027 after Monday's transactions, though the Jaguars still hold their first-round picks in 2028 and 2029.

The Jaguars have been to the playoffs only twice in the past nine seasons, and they fell in the wild-card round in 2025, the first under head coach Liam Coen's watch. While Monday's transactions were undoubtedly costly for the franchise, the Jaguars feel ready to go for it and make a legitimate postseason run. Whether that is enough for them to run the table in the AFC and make what would be the team's first Super Bowl remains to be seen.

#2932. Puka Nacua injury will cause Rams' WR to miss extended time

Los Angeles Rams wide receiver Puka Nacua will likely go on injured reserve and miss a minimum of four games with multiple injuries, head coach Sean McVay told reporters on Monday, Nov. 9.

Nacua is dealing with sprains to both his PCL and MCL and is also dealing with turf toe, McVay said.

"He's gonna be out for a good amount of time, will probably be a guy that we'll put on (injured reserve) as a result of that," McVay told reporters.

Nacua underwent an MRI on Monday as the Rams were already thin at receiver, with Davante Adams on the shelf for a few more weeks at minimum with a hamstring injury.

Nacua carried the Rams to a 20-13 victory over the Washington Commanders on Sunday, Nov. 8. He had 14 catches for 150 yards and a touchdown and had 19 targets, along with a pair of rushing attempts (for 20 yards). He was targeted 24 times in the Rams' overtime loss to the Buffalo Bills on Oct. 12.

Nacua entered the season with an ailing groin that cost him Weeks 2 and 3. He suffered this knee injury on the final play of the game against the Commanders.

Nacua has scored in four of his last five games (including three straight) and is seventh in the NFL in receiving yards (715) and tied for third in catches (70).

The Rams' next four games are at the Arizona Cardinals, home against the Green Bay Packers (Thanksgiving Eve) and Kansas City Chiefs and on the road for a divisional game against the San Francisco 49ers on Dec. 13. A Week 11 bye for the Rams means Nacua can return before the Rams host the Cowboys on Dec. 20.

Without Nacua and Adams, the Rams' receiving room will have to be added to – either via practice-squad elevation, free-agent signing or before the Tuesday afternoon trade deadline. Here's a look at where the position group stands:

The good news for quarterback Matthew Stafford and Rams fans is McVay's preference for using multiple tight-end sets within his offense.

#2929. Congress returns with government funding, Pete Hegseth and the debt limit on the agenda

The House and Senate are due to return to Washington on Monday with a crowded legislative agenda in the final weeks before the end of the 119th Congress.

Congress will need to act quickly to extend government funding, which expires Dec. 11. Lawmakers will need to pass the National Defense Authorization Act, and they may tackle ambitious proposals including a construction permitting package and a bill to regulate name, image and likeness rules in college sports.

The work will commence under a new dynamic. Republicans come back to the Capitol diminished after suffering substantial losses in this week's midterms. Democrats swept to a commanding majority in the House with at least 255 seats in the 435-member chamber and could emerge with as many as 56 seats in the Senate.

With majorities in both chambers looming, Democrats will have little incentive to cut deals with their GOP counterparts.

The most pressing concern lawmakers will face when they return is funding the government. Current funding expires Dec. 11. Congress must pass another continuing resolution to retain current spending levels or a full-year suite of appropriations bills before then to avoid a government shutdown.

Appropriations bills and continuing resolutions require bipartisan majorities due to the Senate's 60-vote filibuster threshold. Democrats are unlikely to negotiate a full year of government funding before the new Congress is sworn in, given that they will soon hold commanding majorities in both chambers and can use that to leverage concessions from the White House.

"This former preschool teacher is going to teach President Trump a simple lesson: The power of the purse belongs to CONGRESS!" Sen. Patty Murray, D-Wash., the presumptive chair of the Appropriations Committee, said in an X post Tuesday as election results rolled in. "It's NOT Trump's money. Taxpayer dollars belong to the American PEOPLE."

Adding another wrinkle to the funding push is the electoral loss of Sen. Susan Collins, R-Maine, who chairs the Senate Appropriations Committee and is one of the "four corners" of spending talks. Collins lost reelection to Democrat Troy Jackson, ending a five-term career in the Senate.

Some lawmakers, including Sen. Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., have called for Congress to raise the debt limit before Republicans lose control.

In September, the federal debt hit $40 trillion for the first time, bumping up against the $41.1 trillion ceiling written into the GOP's 2025 budget reconciliation law known as the "one big beautiful bill."

The Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonprofit that tracks the debt limit, said Treasury is likely to hit its maximum borrowing capacity by mid-summer. The U.S. may no longer be able to meet all its obligations by November 2027, according to a Bank of America estimate from Nov. 6.

But Democrats may instead prefer to use the debt limit as a bargaining chip in the next Congress.

Republicans could theoretically raise the debt ceiling in a budget reconciliation package, as they did with their 2025 law. But cobbling together a post-election coalition to clear another reconciliation bill with exceptionally narrow margins would be difficult. Budget reconciliation, which allows party-line bills to skirt the filibuster in the Senate, requires near-total unity.

Congressional leadership, particularly on the Republican side, is likely to have difficulty with lawmaker attendance after dozens of members lost their seats in an electoral bloodbath. Leadership will also struggle to whip members into line, especially those who do not have another election to run.

Top senators on energy committees struck a deal on a permitting bill before the election. That accord to make construction easier could now be in jeopardy, though the Senate plans to hold a procedural vote to advance it Monday evening, according to a Senate aide familiar with the schedule. The person asked not to be named because the move has not been announced.

The resounding majorities Democrats won in both the House and Senate may give them incentive to torpedo the permitting deal in hopes of securing a better one later. The deal also may have been in trouble prior to the election, with top Democrats in the House not supporting the version their counterparts in the Senate accepted.

Politico reported Thursday that Democratic Minority Leader and likely next House Speaker Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., did not support the permitting deal.

Lawmakers have long sought, and failed to reach, a bipartisan, bicameral deal on permitting reform. Democrats want to speed up the buildout of clean energy. Republicans have long complained that the federal permitting process and environmental laws that comprise it stymie the U.S.'s ability to build critical infrastructure.

The House passed a version of the NDAA, which would authorize more than $1 trillion in defense spending, in July. But Senate Democrats blocked their chamber's version of the bill that same month over concerns about the Iran war.

"Donald Trump is dragging America deeper into a war in Iran with no authorization, no plan, and no exit strategy. Democrats will not go along," Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., said in a statement at the time.

The NDAA is typically seen as a must-pass, bipartisan bill.

The legislation as it stood in July also drew pushback from businesses over a provision that would restrict Pentagon contractors from executing stock buybacks and paying dividends unless they have the Defense Department's approval.

A new wrinkle threatens to derail the NDAA process during the lame duck, however. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth faces a vote on articles of impeachment upon Congress' return, and Democrats have already been highly skeptical of the Pentagon's asks in this year's budget as the expensive Iran war drags on.

Other priorities could also get floor time.

The Senate in September approved, in a bipartisan vote, a package that would establish federal rules for the ways in which college athletes are compensated. But that legislation has many opponents in the House, particularly among Democrats in the chamber, making its passage more complicated.

There's also been a yearslong effort to ban members of Congress from owning or trading individual stocks.

In previous Congresses, the effort was stymied by leadership from both parties. And more recently, a bipartisan push to pass legislation was derailed after House Democrats demanded the ban also apply to the president.

One GOP-led proposal, called the Stop Insider Trading Act, passed the House with some bipartisan support in July, but met with Democratic opposition because it would apply only to Congress and would not require lawmakers to divest existing holdings.

Sen. Todd Young, R-Ind., called for action on the issue in an X post Thursday. Young is a co-sponsor of the Senate version of the Stop Insider Trading Act.

"We have a great opportunity in the lame duck session to restore a small amount of trust by banning Members of Congress from trading stocks. I've been talking about this for many months, and it should be a no-brainer," Young said.

Democrats, however, are eager to claim a victory on congressional stock trading in the next Congress.

Jeffries, in a letter to fellow House members announcing his candidacy for speaker, listed it as a priority as part of Democrats' anti-corruption agenda.

"We must root out corrupt price gouging, clean up the mess at the Supreme Court and ban Members of Congress from trading stocks. Accountability begins at home," Jeffries wrote.

#2928. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2926. Home listings pile up in October, but sellers won't slash prices

Homebuyers continue to retreat, but sellers are still digging in, refusing to cut prices or give up. As a result, September's frozen housing market is now October's frozen and increasingly bloated market.

Active inventory in October hit 183,500 homes, up 7.4% year-over-year, according to Realtor.com. That is the fastest annual growth in eight months. Active listings have been rising each month since February. This is despite the fact that new listings are actually falling, down 7.5% from October 2025.

The market has seen several years of extremely lean supply, driven by the homebuying frenzy during the first two years of the pandemic, when mortgage rates hit more than a dozen record lows. Now supply nationally is just 6.6% below typical pre-pandemic levels, the narrowest gap since the pandemic.

"The market is cooling on both sides, but in different ways. Buyers are stepping back as higher mortgage rates cut into their buying power, while sellers are listing less often without a broad increase in delistings or price cuts," said Danielle Hale, chief economist at Realtor.com. "We're seeing a pause in new supply, but owners who already have homes on the market appear to be largely unphased."

Just over 20% of sellers cut prices in October, about the same as both September and October of last year. There was also no meaningful increase in delistings by sellers.

Mortgage rates came down a little bit in October month-to-month, but they are still over a full percentage point higher than a year ago. That continued to weigh on sales. Newly pending sales, representing signed contracts, not closings, dropped 16.8% in October year-over-year, according to Zillow. That was the biggest annual drop since August 2023 and slowest October since 2018.

While rates fell slightly, home prices continued to gain. Zillow estimated the monthly mortgage payment on the typical U.S. home was $2,004 in October, assuming a 20% down payment and excluding taxes and insurance. That is an increase of 12.8% from last year and 4.8% higher than in September, the biggest one-month jump in four years.

"November is shaping up to have an even larger contraction, but the silver lining is that next month is likely going to be the worst of it," said Mischa Fisher, Zillow's chief economist, in a release. "I expect the pace of the slowdown to ease into the new year. There are deals to be had for buyers able to brave the affordability headwinds in the coming months, and the continued inventory buildup means there is a lot of upside for sales into next year if we get rate relief."

#2924. The Celtics are replacing Jaylen Brown by committee

Since teaming up in the fall of 2017, Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum forged on in Beantown as the Boston Celtics shuffled through name after name around them.

Kyrie Irving. Gordon Hayward. Kemba Walker. Robert Williams III. Marcus Smart. Al Horford. Jrue Holiday. Kristaps Porzingis.

All of them came and went while Boston pursued a title — and eventually claimed one in emphatic fashion — behind its two young, then budding, and, finally, established stars on the wing.

A year after winning a championship in 2024, the Tatum-Brown-Holiday-Horford-Porzingis core dissolved as the latter three departed. Despite Tatum missing much of 2025-26 while recovering from a torn Achilles, the Celtics pivoted toward a new group around their two pillars. Then, after a bountiful nine-year partnership, Tatum and Brown split.

In exchange for Paul George and two first-round picks (and two swaps), Boston dealt Brown to the Philadelphia 76ers over the summer. Brown's exit represented the Celtics' most dramatic roster reshaping in recent years. Sure, the year prior marked a wider exodus of names but none of them helped define Boston's vision and identity like Brown.

Through nine games, that's proven true on both ends of the floor. The Celtics have altered how they elect to deploy their offensive figureheads and spotlight George's defensive splendor — the product of Brown's absence (for better and worse) and George's arrival (for better and worse).

Without Brown, Boston does not sport a creator equipped to soak up mammoth on-ball volume beyond Tatum, who sports a career-high 36% usage rate (third league-wide, per Cleaning the Glass) and is averaging 27.9 points, 8.0 assists and 6.3 rebounds per game this season.

But the Celtics have not spiked Tatum's responsibilities by simply handing him the ball up top and demanding he stir more of their drink. His 27.2% on-ball rate is lower than in 2025-26 (28.3%) and 2024-25 (28.8%) while his 20.0 creation true shot attempts per 100 possessions (pick-and-rolls, isolation, post-ups) align with the previous two years as well (19.0, 19.4), per databallr.

Instead, they're leveraging his size, strength and fluidity to garner advantageous touches inside, dive to the rim around off-ball screens and attack tilted defenses on the move. A 33% rim frequency is his highest since 2022-23 and tied for the second-gaudiest mark of his career.

Tatum relishes contact these days and will bludgeon through any defender to finish or draw fouls. His .510 free-throw rate is obliterating a previous career-high of .399. Only Luka Doncic (102) and Giannis Antetokounmpo (83) have attempted more free throws than his 80 this season.

He is huge, strong and emboldened. The Celtics are wielding that to their benefit.

Plays like those emphasize another crucial detail in how Tatum's reached such towering usage: teammates are setting up him more often. Forty-seven percent of all his field goals are assisted, his highest rate since 2022-23, including 53% at the rim and 41% from midrange — also his highest rates since 2022-23, per Cleaning the Glass.

In contrast to their guard-sized contemporaries, wing-sized initiators often incur drawbacks with their burst, handle and flexibility. They are bigger and stronger, so they are slower and less adept maneuvering through tight windows. Yet they are bigger and stronger than their diminutive peers, traits that present their own upshots. As best they can, the Celtics are embracing the upshots to help mitigate the drawbacks while still bestowing Tatum cream-of-the-crop usage.

He is logging plenty of traditional creation reps, though, too. And when he is commandeering Boston's offense, he's looked spectacular as a facilitator. His 40.5% assist rate dwarfs his previous career-high of 26.5%.

His 16.7 potential assists per 100 are also a career high, besting his 15.8 in 2024-25. When he returned last season, he was mighty punctual and precise as a distributor, reliably dissecting opponents if they sent too many limbs into his orbit. That's continued this year.

Most notably, he's amended his playmaking patterns. Whereas he's typically erred for kickout passes to shooters against gap help or blitzes — partly because he's played with many below-the-rim and/or floor-spacing centers — he's prioritizing more interior reads this season and averaging a career-high 5.2 at-rim assists per 100 possessions (99th percentile).

That shatters last year's career-high of 3.0, which already trounced his then-career-high of 2.2 in 2024-25. Boston's rim frequency is 9.3% higher with him on the floor this season (per Cleaning the Glass), the result of his interior-oriented style and preferences as a passer.

Whether he's playing alongside Neemias Queta or Mitchell Robinson, he's utilizing his 84-inch acrobats to generate easy shots as the Celtics' primary initiator. His own height empowers him to disregard extra attention over the top and he's increasingly manipulating help defenders with his eyes to open up passes inside, particularly when George or Sam Hauser — Boston's most vaunted off-ball shooters — are stationed in the weak-side corner.

In totality, Tatum receiving newfound off-ball opportunities is a positive for his game. But a focus operating downhill and starting touches inside the arc has somewhat damaged his shot profile. According to Cleaning the Glass, 19% of his field goals are long midrange attempts (14 feet to the 3-point line) and 35% of his field goals fall under the broader midrange designation (beyond 4 feet to the 3-point line). The former is highest since 2018-19 and the latter is his highest since 2020-21.

Of course, there are a handful of voluminous scorers who flourish in the intermediate region. Tatum is not among them. He's shooting 39% from midrange and has only eclipsed 40% once in his decade-long NBA tenure.

Too routinely, he will catch the ball in the post or against a switch and squander the chance with an arduous pull-up 2, turnaround jumper or fadeaway. He's shooting 51.5% inside the arc, his worst mark since 2020-21, when he shot 52.4% — last year's abbreviated, post-injury stretch excluded.

An uptick in looks around the rim and from midrange has seen Tatum's 3-point rate dwindle to 30%, his lowest mark since 2018-19. And he's been anemic from deep thus far, shooting 28.6%. That should perk up as the sample swells and 51.5% on 2s isn't such a deviation from recent years that any long-range pulse, when paired with his premier foul-drawing emergence, should allow him to approximate or exceed the 60% true shooting he accrued from 2022-23 to 2024-25 (a very respectable 58% this year).

If Tatum has not suddenly devolved into a Thompson Twins-level outside shooter, the tweaks to his usage and passing upswing should set the stage for a career-best campaign offensively. And he is not the only Celtics wing embarking on a new offensive journey.

Boston recognizes it requires a substantial role for George offensively but cannot just hand him Brown's shoes and tell him to fit in them. He is nowhere near the shot creator or explosive driver of Brown but does have some advantages, such as size, versatile off-ball play and passing.

So, head coach Joe Mazzulla is constantly rotating George through various jobs to influence possessions without overtaxing him on the ball — and the early returns are excellent. He's averaging 19.6 points (58% true shooting), 5.5 rebounds, 4.5 assists and 2.4 steals while shooting 41.3% beyond the arc.

Despite a career-high 75% of his field goals coming via assists, he touts a 27.6% usage rate, his highest since 2022-23, and is averaging 9.1 creation true shooting attempts per 100, his lowest in the stat's database spanning back to 2013-14; his time of possession is also its lowest since 2017-18, per NBA.com. Similar to Tatum, Boston is granting George touches in favorable contexts by leaning on its blend of elite screening, decision-making and spacing around them.

As George has aged, he's lost much of his spunk as a driver. That physical decline has resigned him into many challenging midrange jumpers over defenders who he can no longer scoot past or chisel through to the rim. The Celtics don't want that as a basis of their offense, so they're implementing plenty of actions that manufacture advantages for George, whether it be off-ball screens to help him pressure the paint, forcing smaller defenders onto him or letting him catch the ball on the move to ease his creation burden.

His .508 3-point rate is a career high and he's hoisting a career-high 13.0 triples per 100 possessions. They're also utilizing him as a screener and roller more than any other team has in his career. According to databallr, his 3.7 finishing true shooting attempts (cuts, putbacks, rolls) per 100 is a career-high. Related: a 21% rim frequency is his highest since 2022-23. Boston's offense is helping George optimize his shot profile and he's taking his fewest share of midrange jumpers (31%) since 2011-12.

Reinvigorating George and Tatum as forces inside — and Tatum's playmaking pivot — feels like an intentional effort to replace Brown's driving prowess. The Celtics' 30.2% rim frequency is their highest since 2021-22 and ranks 16th league-wide, their highest standing since 2019-20. That 30.2% mark is loftier than anything they posted with Brown on the floor over his final three years in Boston, which isn't to frame Brown negatively but to underscore the Celtics' revamped approach offensively.

*Billy Beane voice* Boston is aiming to recreate Brown in the aggregate.

Tatum has commanded a heftier workload and is calling the rim home more often. George is the secondary creator, albeit a lesser one than Brown. Because Tatum's offensive role has expanded to help replace Brown, the Celtics are asking less of him defensively and calling upon George to fill that void and some of the void Brown left behind.

He's assuming Brown's former point-of-attack duties while doubling as an off-ball roamer who can guard bigs (Tatum's tasks) when Queta ignores a non-shooter to patrol the paint. He's third in the NBA in deflections per game (5.3) and fifth in steals (2.4), and owns a career-high STOP% of 4.7 (steals, blocks recovered and offensive fouls drawn per 100).

Just as Boston is highlighting Tatum's enormity on offense, it's doing the same with George on defense. His blend of size, screen navigation, instincts and off-ball disruption puts him in rare air defensively. He is huge and mobile. The Celtics are wielding that to their benefit.

Despite a 7-2 start with their leading men generally thriving, there is much to still sort out and navigate for the Celtics as the season further unfolds. Although I was higher than most on George's entire Sixers tenure and his pre-suspension performance in 2025-26, it is undeniable he played much better last year once returning from a 25-game layoff.

An offseason of recuperation offered a similar reprieve. And his scoring numbers since a 38-point barrage to open the year aren't as rosy: 17.0 points on 53.6% true shooting (37.3% from 3) over the past seven games. How will he fare on both ends as the grind of a long season mounts and transitions to a grueling playoff environment?

As splendid as Tatum's been offensively and wondrous as George has been defensively, I still believe Boston, to etch a deep postseason run, will need more of its franchise superstar defensively. His rim protection, general activity and rebounding on that end are at career-worst levels, the last of which is bleeding into Boston's 24th-ranked defensive rebounding rate (it's never finished worse than 11th in the Mazzulla era).

Perhaps, that is a conscious decision from him and the coaching staff, given his prolific offensive scope. But he is, ideally, capable of more. Will he dial it up at some point? Can he? It's a ton to ask, particularly because I don't believe this offensive setting has an off-ramp for him to effectively scale back.

And for as cohesive and cunning as the Celtics' offense is, I'm unsure of its playoff translation. They win on the margins with early flare screens, back-screens and drag screens that catch less-alert defenses off guard in the regular season — all details Tatum and George have ridden to early success.

When the stakes are raised in the postseason, countering schematic advantages is easier than countering talent advantages. The 2025-26 and 2026-27 Celtics have typically won because of the former, not the latter; their title team merged both components for dominance. This iteration lacks the ball-handling abundance and malleable center play the 2023-24 and 2024-25 squads enjoyed.

It is also possible a championship is not Boston's utmost goal or one the organization internally deems plausible this year. Building a team unable to do so during its All-NBA superstar's prime may warrant criticism but is a separate conversation.

This is Tatum's first full year back from a torn Achilles. George, while still very good, is 36 years old and not an All-Star-caliber player anymore. His lucrative contract will be an expiring deal next summer. Derrick White, while a pillar of Boston's prosperous last half-decade, is 32 and potentially trending downward.

Maybe, the Celtics are content to see what this Tatum-led group can achieve before parlaying George's expiring deal and their wealth of first-round picks into a better, younger upgrade over the offseason. Maybe, they continue piling up wins and feel compelled to make a splash at this season's deadline.

Regardless, this team is good but different than years past and still spearheaded by Tatum — who, for the first time in his career, is flanked by a new running mate on the wing.

All stats accurate prior to games played on Nov. 8.

#2919. Bucks Reacts Survey: Did Milwaukee win the Giannis trade?

Welcome to SB Nation Reacts, a survey of fans across the NBA. Throughout the year we ask questions of the most plugged-in Bucks fans and fans across the country.

The old adage proclaims that the team that gets the best player wins the trade. By that logic, the Miami Heat are the clear-cut winners in the trade that sent Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bobby Portis to the Heat in exchange for Tyler Herro, Jaime Jaquez Jr., Kel'el Ware, Kasparas Jakučionis, and picks, including the 13th-overall selection in this year's draft, which ended up being Nate Ament. But is it all that simple?

Miami traded for Giannis on the basis of winning now. Three weeks into the season, that's not really happening, with the Heat going 4-6 and sitting eighth in the Eastern Conference standings (moving up after a narrow win over the Sacramento Kings last night). Moreover, the Heat were billed as a defensive-juggernaut-to-be, yet that reality couldn't be further from the truth. At this point of the season, the Heat rank 26th in defensive rating (116.8), 27th in opponent points per game (121.1 PPG), and 28th in opponent field goal percentage (48.8%). Their defensive struggles aren't lost on head coach extraordinaire Erik Spoelstra, who said as much himself:

We're losing games because of our defense. That has to be our 1st, 2nd, and 3rd priorities. Everything else we'll start to make some progress.

Others, including ESPN's Brian Windhorst and Tim MacMahon, have noticed too:

Of course, the Milwaukee Bucks have their own problems—plenty of 'em, including being too nice—and, despite being in most of their games thus far, find themselves sitting just 1-9 and in last place.

Individually, Giannis has put up similar numbers to what he did for Milwaukee: 27.5 PPG, 12.8 RPG, 5.3 APG, 1.0 SPG, and 1.1 BPG. However, he's suddenly copping a lot more criticism in the media—a "perk" for requesting a trade to a big market, perhaps. Most notably, former coach Doc Rivers spoke about how Giannis is "not easy" to coach, while former player Nick Young was downright abusive of Giannis on Gil's Arena. Manner aside, both of these point to game-style issues that have long been known, at least around here. Portis, meanwhile, is struggling to find his groove in South Beach, averaging just 7.0 PPG and 6.3 RPG on .371/.325/.714 shooting splits.

Closer to home, the Bucks' recruits have been a mixed bag. Herro has been great, air-frying his way to 21.4 PPG, 4.6 RPG, and 4.3 APG and providing rare veteran stability for a young team trying to figure things out (including how to go from competing to winning). Jakučionis has shown strong glimpses, as has Ament, who is likely ahead of the curve many projected for him. Jaquez has also been a positive, arguably playing the best ball of his career, although his long-term tenure on the team is anything but guaranteed having not come to terms on a rookie-scale extension. Then there's Ware, who many saw as the prize acquisition and a potential breakout candidate, but who has been underwhelming to say the least, exhibiting many of the concerning habits that came to light during his time in Miami—and who seems to be finding a home in Taylor Jenkins' doghouse.

For Miami, the goal of the trade is clear: win a championship. Pat Riley owned this, saying "We are here to win. Giannis wants to win. Our plan is to go for it right from the beginning." Giannis did too:

This puts a premium on the present. But if the Heat aren't contending this year, then there's a helluva lot of pressure to win next year—when Giannis will be turning 33—otherwise it might just be too late. For at least one fan, this pressure, not to mention the impending contract pressure when Giannis' extension options become more lucrative in January, is not missed.

For Milwaukee, the play is obviously long term. Bereft of many (any?) real assets prior to the trade, this was a chance for the Bucks to build anew. And, fingers and toes—and anything else you can—crossed that the Bucks aren't punished for the Gary Trent Jr. deal, they are in a better position to build than they would have been rehashing the same plan as yesteryear. Right?

So, three-or-so weeks into the season, now that we've seen how the acquisitions and teams look, how do you evaluate the trade?

Let us know your thoughts by voting below—and don't forget to explain your reasons in the comments.

#2916. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2915. Many workers lose unused FSA money — but could still come out ahead. Here's how

Health flexible spending accounts, or FSAs, are tax-advantaged accounts that let workers set aside pretax money to cover eligible health expenses. However, they're often use it or lose it, with unspent balances forfeited at the end of the plan year.

About 4 in 10 workers with health FSAs forfeited unused money in 2024, according to an October report from the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonprofit research organization. Among those FSA account holders, the average forfeiture was $404, down from $436 in 2023. The research examined more than 3.3 million accounts representing $3.9 billion in contributions.

Yet even workers who don't zero out their balance may still benefit from using an FSA, compared with what they would have spent using after-tax dollars to cover the same expenses.

"Even if someone forfeits FSA funds back to their employer, they can still come out ahead — relative to not participating in an FSA — if the amount they forfeited is less than the tax savings they realized," said Jake Spiegel, a senior research associate for EBRI and author of the study.

Nearly three-quarters — 72% — of state and local government workers and 47% of private-sector workers have access to a health FSA in 2026, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That compares with 68% and 40%, respectively, in 2017.

FSAs differ from health savings accounts, or HSAs, which some people use in conjunction with high-deductible health plans. Although both let you set aside pretax money for health care expenses, HSAs let you roll over your balance from year to year, and even invest it.

Roughly a third of FSA users in the EBRI database face a strict use-it-or-lose-it provision, Spiegel said. The rest either are permitted by their employers to carry over an amount — up to $680 in 2026 — into the following year or are offered a grace period of up to 2.5 months after the deadline to incur additional expenses or face forfeiture. However, employers cannot offer both options.

While many plans have a Dec. 31 deadline for spending FSA money, some employers have deadlines falling in other parts of the year, said Rachel Rouleau, chief compliance officer for Health-E Commerce, parent company of online retailers FSA Store and HSA Store.

"Understanding these plan-specific deadlines is essential, so employees should check with their employer or FSA administrator to avoid forfeiting unused funds," Rouleau said.

Additionally, many employers give you extra time — typically three months after the deadline — to submit receipts for qualifying expenses that occurred during the previous plan year.

For 2026, the maximum contribution allowed to health FSAs is $3,400. In 2027, that amount will be $3,500, the IRS announced Thursday.

Part of the reason workers have forfeitures may be that they must decide how much they want to contribute to their FSA before the plan year begins.

"It's difficult to project how much you'll need to spend on health care, especially if you are particularly healthy and don't have a chronic condition with predictable prescription drug costs," Spiegel said.

Contributions to FSAs are withheld from paychecks and generally avoid federal taxes, as well as so-called payroll taxes — known as FICA — that go toward Social Security and Medicare. They also may avoid state and local taxes, depending on where you live.

To determine how much you're saving in taxes by using an FSA, you can add up those tax rates and apply the total to your contribution, said certified financial planner Sean Lovison, founder of Purpose Built Financial Services in Moorestown, New Jersey.

For example, say that your federal, FICA, state and local taxes total 35% and you contributed the maximum allowed — $3,400 for 2026. In this case, the calculation would result in $1,190 in estimated tax savings, which is "how much you're saving on taxes by contributing to your FSA," Lovison said.

The illustration assumes the worker receives no employer contribution to the FSA and the tax rate applies to the entire contribution.

"As long as the number you're forfeiting from your FSA is less than that number, you're going to come out ahead," Lovison said.

Of course, he said, "you don't want to walk away from FSA money, but at the same time, from a mathematical standpoint, you're still coming out ahead."

If you face a forfeiture, you can use it to help inform your decision on how much to put toward your FSA for 2027, if you haven't already made benefit elections for next year.

"Employees should look at their anticipated healthcare expenses over the coming year and consider both their daily health routines and expected annual expenses," Rouleau said. "This includes recurring prescriptions, doctor visits, copays, deductibles, dental and vision care, and other eligible out-of-pocket healthcare expenses for themselves and their eligible dependents."

Before you resign yourself to a forfeiture, keep in mind that there are also many ways to use the money on eligible expenses before the end of the year.

"There are thousands of ways consumers can responsibly spend down their FSA dollars, from everyday healthcare essentials to eligible telehealth services," Rouleau said.

Additionally, "many consumers are surprised to learn that a wide range of over-the-counter medications, menstrual care products, sunscreen, first-aid supplies and certain health-monitoring devices are FSA-eligible," Rouleau said.

That's in addition to doctor and dentist appointments, prescription drugs and other healthcare services such as acupuncture and addiction treatment, according to the IRS.

If you're unsure whether you have money remaining in your FSA or need to spend it by the end of the year, most plans offer an online portal where you can review your balance, submit claims and check deadlines. The FSA administrator's information is typically listed on the back of your FSA card, Rouleau said.

#2913. Mariners look forward and back in current coaching staff hires

The Mariners made the hires of bench coach Tanner Swanson and hitting coach CJ Gillman official today, continuing a theme that started when the hiring of manager Jason Kanzler was officially announced last week: the youth movement is coming back to Seattle.

In Dipoto's first season, the Mariners trended towards conservative hires or retaining coaches who had already been in the organization. Dipoto brought over several people he'd worked with during his time with the Angels – Mike Micucci, Ethan Katz, and Zac Livingston – and retained most other staff members rather than cleaning house organizationally from top to bottom.

But by 2016, Dipoto and then-Director of Player Development Andy McKay were ready to take some bigger swings. One of the biggest was hiring a 32-year-old Mitch Canham to manage their A-ball affiliate, the Clinton LumberKings. At the time, Canham was well-known to Northwest baseball fans as a standout star at Lake Stevens high school and then Oregon State, but an unknown quantity in the broader coaching world, lacking any official managerial experience before being handed the reins at a full-season affiliate.

Canham's hiring was part of an organizational philosophy shift that emphasized quality of leadership over depth of experience – as McKay put it, "better people make better baseball players", which starts with them being led by the best people available. Joining Canham in Clinton was pitching coach Pete Woodworth, who had more official coaching experience but was just 28 years old at the time.

Those hires sparked an influx of young coaching talent, often from non-traditional coaching backgrounds, into the Mariners organization. The next few years would see the hiring of at the time relatively unknown names, many of whom who have since moved on from Seattle to build impressive coaching resumes elsewhere: Tony Arnerich, Carson Vitale, Hugh Quattlebaum, Max Weiner, Jarret DeHart, Rob Marcello, Sean McGrath, just to name a handful. Those coaches oversaw the transition of the Mariners farm system from one of the worst in baseball to a consistent solid performer, and the pitching coaches especially helped the Mariners earn a well-deserved reputation as one of the sport's best in developing pitchers.

But the Mariners paid the price – or rather, didn't – for that innovation, losing many of their young coaches to better opportunities (and/or better salaries) elsewhere. Two that stung the most were the losses of Canham and Weiner, both of whom moved on to high-profile (and high-paid) jobs at the college level. The annual turnover started disrupting the chain of development, and the ties that bound the minor-league process to the big-league results-oriented product weakened until the cracks showed through clearly in 2026: an organization that seemed to drift without an identity or plan.

Jerry Dipoto was frank in his end-of-season presser about acknowledging the disconnect between the process the organization worked so hard to implement at the minor-league level and the big leagues, and the necessity of hiring someone who can get things back on the right track.

"We will find someone through this process who comes in and is in alignment with what we see as the direction this team needs to head in, and then guides us in a way that we get better," said Dipoto, prior to Kanzler's hiring. "Because obviously the performance on the field this year has made us question a lot of the things that we're doing at the major-league level, and I will say multiple of our major-league players who came through our system have referenced what they learned in player development that got abandoned in the big leagues this year…We need to get back to what we believe is a Mariners baseball process.

In the press conference introducing Kanzler, Justin Hollander concurred with what Dipoto had said a month earlier.

"We need to be better about connecting what we believe in organizationally to what we do in the big leagues. That's something that was identified by Jerry, myself, our baseball operations staff, and most importantly, our players as we went through the end of the season," said Hollander.

"We also need to push ourselves, try new things that maybe hadn't been on our radar. So finding someone with Jason's skillset that can develop while we're in the big leagues, that can connect an organization from top to bottom, was a huge part of this."

The Mariners of the last few years of the 2010s weren't afraid to push the envelope, to make out-of-the-box hires, and to try new things – at least as far as development-wise. Now, with hiring Kanzler, Gillman, and Swanson – ages 36, 37, and 44, respectively – they're pushing the envelope on the top rail of the dugout.

But for all the seeming risk, it's still a calculated one. Rather than make the clubhouse fit the skipper, the Mariners have chosen a skipper who fits the clubhouse.

"The uniqueness of [Kanzler's] developmental path is going to go a long way with modern players," said Jerry Dipoto.

For all the foofaraw around Kanzler's age and experience, though – or that of his bench coach or hitting coordinator – when Kanzler talks, it feels like an echo of the process the Mariners used to hire Canham a decade ago: getting the best out of players by putting the best, bigger-picture focused people in front of them.

"It's important to have connection with the players," said Kanzler in his opening presser. "It's most important to have connection throughout the entire thing. The entire organization needs to be connected towards the common goal of winning the World Series…I like to think that the sign of things going well is when people start using the word 'we' more than 'me' or 'I.' When the group starts talking in 'we', then I know that things are going well."

#2912. These states are offering extra ACA premium subsidies in 2027

Health care coverage in some states is going to get slightly more affordable in 2027.

At least 11 states are providing funding to Obamacare enrollees to reduce their monthly insurance premiums, which qualifying enrollees should see when they sign up for coverage this open enrollment season.

The move follows a lapse of enhanced federal premium subsidies in 2025 that drove up costs for millions of people who buy health plans on the Affordable Care Act marketplace. The states include California, Colorado, Connecticut, Maryland, Massachusetts, New Mexico, New Jersey, Rhode Island, Vermont, Virginia and Washington state.

Most of the states are continuing policies they put in place for 2026, in the aftermath of the loss of the federal subsidies. But Rhode Island and Virginia are debuting their respective programs for 2027, while other states like California expanded eligibility to additional consumers.

Health policy experts said the state funding could help prevent more consumers from dropping their insurance coverage, following a significant drop decline this year. It also comes amid a broader affordability crunch for U.S. households, which voters cited as a top issue in the recent midterm elections.

While enhanced federal premium subsidies lapsed at the end of 2025, there's still a tranche of federal subsidies — known as premium tax credits — that remain in place. The funding offered by these 11 states comes on top of any federal subsidies consumers may receive.

The amount of financial assistance varies by state, and each state has different eligibility criteria. Many target their programs at lower-income consumers, experts said.

"This was an effort to make coverage more affordable again" for some people, said Louise Norris, a health policy analyst for insurance referral site healthinsurance.org. "It can certainly be significant if you qualify for the help your state provides."

The states offering additional premium assistance operate state-based health insurance marketplaces rather than HealthCare.gov. They're also largely Democratic-leaning states, health policy experts said.

Most offered premium assistance to state residents in 2026. However, Rhode Island and Virginia are new to the list for 2027.

In many of the states, officials said the move to offer state-funded premium assistance is a direct response to the lapse of enhanced ACA premium subsidies at the end of 2025.

After the lapse of the enhanced subsidies — which had been in effect since 2021 and helped push ACA enrollment to record highs — Obamacare premiums increased by an average of 58%, to $178 per month on average in 2026 from $113 per month the prior year, according to KFF, a nonpartisan health policy research group.

Democrats pushed to renew the enhanced premium subsidies — the crux of a government shutdown in 2025 that lasted more than a month — but the Republican-led Congress opted not to extend them.

ACA enrollment fell to 19.2 million as of February 2026, a 13% decline from 22.1 million in February 2025, according to figures published in June by the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services.

Evidence suggests enrollment has fallen further since then, according to a KFF analysis.

Health policy experts largely attribute the decline to the expiration of enhanced subsidies, since the lapse pushed up costs for consumers.

"Where Congress has failed, Colorado is stepping up," Gov. Jared Polis said in September in a press release discussing insurance premium assistance. "Despite the loss of the enhanced premium tax credits, we are doing everything we can to keep health insurance prices down so Coloradans can afford access to health care."

Not all consumers enrolled in an ACA marketplace health plan will be eligible for their state's financial assistance.

Of all the states, New Mexico is the only one that is fully replacing the enhanced federal premium subsidies for ACA enrollees in the state in 2027, extending a policy in place for 2026, according to health policy experts.

While ACA enrollment declined nationally in 2026, New Mexico was the only state to see an increase in its enrollment figures, growing 14% between 2025 and 2026, according to KFF.

Some states allocated more funding to their premium assistance programs for 2027 relative to 2026.

In California, for example, Gov. Gavin Newsom, a Democrat, and the California Legislature increased the amount of state funds available for the Covered California State Subsidy Program to $300 million in 2027 from $190 million in 2026, according to a press release on the state's health insurance marketplace.

Californians with incomes up to 200% of the federal poverty level in 2027 — $31,920 for an individual or $66,000 for a family of four — will be eligible for a state premium subsidy. In 2026, those subsidies are available for households with incomes up to 165% of the federal poverty line.

As a result of the eligibility expansion, an additional 200,000 will be eligible for financial help who weren't eligible in 2026, Covered California, the state marketplace, said in a press release.

Massachusetts also increased its investment in financial assistance for ACA enrollees to $620 million in 2027 from $600 million this year — the largest state investment in the country, state officials said in October.

The value of state assistance can be modest for some consumers, depending on factors like their income and monthly premiums, health policy experts said.

For example, in Washington state, ACA enrollees with an income up to 200% of the federal poverty line and who receive a federal subsidy can get a state-funded subsidy of up to $35 per month. Those with an income between 201% and 250% of the poverty line can get up to $20 per month.

That said, states that offer premium subsidies saw a lower decline in ACA marketplace enrollment than in states that didn't offer any financial assistance, experts said.

"We saw that states in 2026 that did offer assistance had smaller declines in enrollment," said Justin Lo, a senior researcher at KFF who specializes in the Affordable Care Act. "If the previous pattern continues, we will also see possibly better enrollment retention in states that are more generous with their subsidies."

It's not a guarantee states will be able to continue funding these subsidies into the future, experts said.

"Funding is obviously the crux of the issue," said Norris, of healthinsurance.org. "Whether states will be able to continue to provide funding for these programs really depends on these states' budgets."
