#2911. Anthropic releases first frontier models since Amodei's call for slowdown as IPO buzz builds

Anthropic on Monday released two new artificial intelligence models, Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2, as the company readies for a potentially historic IPO that could take place as soon as next week.

Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2 are Anthropic's most advanced offerings, and the company said they generate outputs 30% faster than their predecessors. The models also cost 20% less than the 5.1 generation, and will be available for $8 per million input tokens and $40 per million output tokens, the company said.

The launch marks the first time that Anthropic has released so-called "frontier" models since its CEO, Dario Amodei, called to slow down the pace of AI development in September. Anthropic said the models went through rigorous safety and alignment testing, and that the company developed new evaluations and post-launch monitoring tools ahead of the release.

"We called for pacing, not pausing," Dianne Penn, Anthropic's head of product management for research, told CNBC in an interview.

Anthropic has to balance its safety efforts with intense commercial and competitive pressures, a tension that the company highlighted in the extensive risk factors section of its prospectus late last month. And as executives prepare to kick off Anthropic's IPO roadshow, they'll have to convince investors that their approach is the right one.

The company is aiming for a valuation of $1 trillion to $1.5 trillion in its offering, as CNBC previously reported, down from a target of $2 trillion that had been floated in recent months.

Anthropic first introduced Mythos in April, and it limited the rollout to a select group of organizations because of the model's advanced cybersecurity capabilities. The company introduced Fable, a publicly available version of Mythos with additional safeguards, two months later.

The models — particularly Mythos — captivated Wall Street and government officials alike, and they helped to fuel Anthropic's meteoric rise this year. The company said in its amended prospectus that it generated $33.24 billion in revenue for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year.

Penn said Fable 5.2 is primarily intended for "power users or power use cases" since it excels at complex, open-ended technical work. Other models like Claude Opus 5.5, which Anthropic released in September, are better suited for daily tasks, so Penn said users should turn to Fable 5.2 when they need to tackle hard problems in engineering, mathematics and robotics.

"These are just net new things that are completely novel, and so they're frontier use cases," Penn said. "We expect that over time to maybe trickle to the Opus class, but really right now we do see a difference in terms of performance that we think those frontier use cases benefit from."

Anthropic said Fable 5.2 is generally available on Monday, and Mythos 5.2 is available to vetted organizations through the company's Life Sciences and Cyber Verification Programs.

#2910. Elizabeth Warren set to take over the Senate banking committee. Here's what she plans to do

Sen. Elizabeth Warren is set to take over the Senate Banking Committee. The Massachusetts Democrat is already charting the panel's course.

Among Warren's top issues for the Congress that convenes in January are addressing the cost of living, tightening export controls on advanced artificial intelligence chips to China and promulgating regulations for prediction markets, according to a Warren banking committee aide. The aide spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss the yet-to-be-announced agenda. Warren is also plotting an aggressive oversight agenda and urged tech CEOs, administration officials and Wall Street executives to preserve their documents, the aide said.

Democrats' dominant win in last week's midterm elections hands them control of committee gavels on Capitol Hill. With that comes the ability to set the legislative agenda for the House and Senate and subpoena power to compel government officials and business leaders to testify under oath. The Banking, Housing and Urban Affairs Committee has a broad remit, including jurisdiction over federal monetary policy, banks and export controls.

Warren's plans to tackle affordability include capping credit card interest rates, following up her 21st Century Road to Housing Act with additional legislation to build more affordable homes and lowering medical, consumer and student debt.

The aide said Warren also intends to put constraints on AI chips going to China, while shaping rules for AI at home.

Warren is also seeking to end the "Wild West-style" lack of oversight in the prediction market industry, the aide said. She and Utah Republican Sen. John Curtis have already written to Kalshi and Polymarket demanding information on safeguards around election-related markets.

The banking committee is also likely to be at the center of Democrats' oversight agenda, which is expected to cast a wide net into private industry. Warren said tech CEOs, administration officials, Trump family cryptocurrency businesses and Wall Street executives should prepare to answer the committee's questions and should preserve their documents, the aide said.

#2908. NFL reinstates WR Brandon Aiyuk from reserve/left squad list

Brandon Aiyuk is back, and the ball is in the 49ers' court.

The 49ers now have an immediate decision to make about Aiyuk. If he's on the roster on Tuesday at 4:00 p.m. ET, he's owed a game check for the week.

Based on the Player Personnel Manual, placement on the reserve/left squad list apparently tolled his 2025 salary. That means he'll return to the $1.17 million base salary from 2025, with the 2026 salary of $26.015 million delayed until 2027.

The 49ers can carry Aiyuk as an extra man on the roster until Monday, November 23.

It's unclear, currently, whether Aiyuk agreed to refund past bonus money to the 49ers in order to get the league to reinstate him. If the 49ers trade or release Aiyuk, the 49ers lose the ability to get money back from Aiyuk.

#2906. Trump administration proposes stricter work-search rules for unemployment benefits

The Labor Department proposed a rule on Monday that would raise the bar for people applying for unemployment insurance by requiring them to prove they've conducted five work-search activities per week in order to qualify for benefits.

In the announcement, the Trump administration described the proposal as a way to improve the "integrity and accountability" of the joint federal-state unemployment insurance system. Consumer advocates said the proposed work-search baseline of five qualifying activities per week exceeded that in most states and would make it more difficult for many applicants to qualify for assistance.

There is currently no uniform requirement across the U.S. for unemployment insurance claimants regarding work-search requirements.

Federal law requires all states to have a work-search requirement, but states have broad discretion to set their own eligibility. They also have ample leeway to set other parameters such as the weekly sum claimants can receive and the number of weeks they can get those funds.

A qualifying work search activity might be applying to a job, creating a profile on a job-search website or attending a networking event, for example, depending on the state.

Currently, only four states require a minimum of five work searches per week, Michele Evermore, the senior director of economic security and worker transitions at the National Employment Law Project, wrote in an email.

The remaining states require fewer activities, she said.

The Labor Department proposal would require individuals claiming unemployment insurance to complete and report at least five qualifying work-search activities each week before payment is issued, according to a press release announcing the rule.

"This proposed rule helps jobseekers find work faster and ensures efficient use of taxpayer resources while improving the overall integrity of our unemployment system," Labor Secretary Keith Sonderling said in the release.

There's a 60-day public comment period on the proposal.

"Unemployment insurance is meant to be temporary support while you find your next job, not a permanent taxpayer-funded paycheck," Sonderling wrote Monday on X.

Jobless workers generally aren't eligible for unemployment insurance for more than six months in almost all states.

The Trump administration proposal amounts to a dramatic change to the unemployment safety net and would make it tougher for unemployment claimants to keep their benefits and increase costs for states, Evermore said.

"Not only is the proposed change devastating to claimants who may not have five search options for every week they are unemployed, but it will likely cost states hundreds of thousands of dollars," Evermore said in an email. "Nearly all states will have to change their claimant intake forms, and with it, their technology — a costly fix. Many states will have to rewrite their laws to meet this rule."

She also said it is "highly unusual for the Department of Labor to unilaterally change how states run their [unemployment insurance] systems absent clear direction from Congress.

The Labor Department rule "strongly encourages" states to allow for "a wide variety of work search activities" to satisfy the requirement, Evermore wrote.

The rule comes in a job market that labor economists have called "low hire, low fire," in which it's difficult for job seekers to find a new gig amid a low national hiring rate and a historically low layoff rate. Altogether, there's lower churn in the job market, contributing to fewer opportunities for job seekers, economists said.

The share of workers who are long-term unemployed — meaning they've been out of work for more than six months — has climbed. About 27.6% of all unemployed workers were considered long-term unemployed as of October, up from 20.5% three years earlier.

#2905. This beaten-down data center stock could be worth a look, says Mike Khouw

I'm a firm believer in investing in what you know—and what you use. During a recent visit to my parents' New Hampshire property, my brother and I tackled overdue maintenance, and having the right equipment on hand was critical to finish the work in the limited time we had.

He and I rented both a compact track loader (CTL, not pictured) and a bulldozer (pictured above) from United Rentals (NYSE: URI) to push through some of the heavier work.

For those who may not be familiar with the company, United Rentals can quickly deploy heavy equipment to a remote site. Here's why I believe it sets up well for a defined-risk, moderately bullish options trade.

The good news is that United Rentals is currently trading at a below-market forward multiple — both on a Price-to-Earnings (P/E) basis, currently ~17.3x forward estimates of ~$61.10/share, and on an Enterprise Value-to-EBITDA (EV/EBITDA) basis at ~8.8x $9.25 billion in FYE2027 EBITDA. This despite the fact that the company is compounding revenues, earnings, and EBITDA at a noticeably faster clip than the cap-weighted S&P 500. Effectively, one can buy superior, market-beating growth at a discounted market multiple.

The recent third-quarter earnings confirmed this trajectory. URI delivered a beat and raise, pointing to robust underlying demand across its end markets, and the sell-side is scrambling to catch up, revising its forward estimates upward. Improving earnings estimates is one of the most reliable, time-tested short-term investment factors. Stocks with positive estimate revisions tend to meaningfully outperform those burdened with declining estimates in the short term.

The macro backdrop provides a sturdy foundation for this growth. We are in the midst of a broader nonresidential construction recovery, which has driven an environment of improving and resilient rental rates. Although admittedly a potential slowdown in data center construction could become a future headwind, broader infrastructure demand remains robust.

A balanced view requires acknowledging the risks. While URI looks cheap relative to the broader market, it is not exactly cheap relative to its own history. The stock is currently trading roughly 20% to 25% above its five-year average multiple. One could easily argue that the historical multiple was simply too cheap for a business of this quality, scale, and capital efficiency. Still, mean reversion back to historical levels of "cheapness" remains a downside risk.

This brings us to the options market. When you have a stock with solid momentum, fundamentals, and positive estimate revisions, but you're hesitant to pay a premium to its historical valuation, outright unhedged equity accumulation isn't always the optimal route. Instead, selling cash-covered puts offers a highly strategic entry mechanism.

With URI's implied volatility currently hovering in the 42% to 45% range, the options market is pricing in enough movement to offer a decent premium. By selling a cash-covered put, we acknowledge both the good and the bad. It respects the aggressive growth, market share capture, and the recent beat-and-raise quarter, while appropriately hedging against the risk of that elevated historical multiple.

If the stock continues its upward march, the puts expire worthless, and we pocket a high-probability yield that easily beats fixed income. If the stock pulls back—perhaps toward that 5-year average multiple—we are assigned the shares at a lower effective cost basis, which, if it happens, we could lower even further by writing upside covered calls against it. (This approach to entering a stock, then writing covered calls if assigned, is sometimes referred to as "the wheel strategy").

So either we acquire an attractive stock at a price we dictate (the short strike, less the premium collected for selling it) or we simply collect premium for assuming that risk if the stock does not decline. For a company that provides the literal heavy lifting for infrastructure growth, that's a structural advantage worth trading.

Disclosures: Tidal owns/holds all the securities mentioned in the article.

#2904. Nvidia’s Trump ties draw scrutiny after reports Huang was targeted by Chinese intelligence

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has spent the past two years cultivating an unparalleled relationship with President Donald Trump, gaining extraordinary access and influence over federal artificial intelligence policy as his company has pushed for looser restrictions on selling advanced chips to China.

Now, revelations about Huang's affair with a suspected Chinese spy are putting that carefully built alliance under scrutiny — along with the millions of dollars Huang and other Nvidia executives have spent expanding the company's political influence in Washington.

The chipmaker is nearly inseparable from its co-founder and CEO Huang, who during Trump's second term has gone from being nearly unknown to the president to being part of a small cadre of executives accompanying him on Air Force One.

Nvidia has committed at least $26 million to Trump's pet projects, including $25 million toward his White House ballroom and $1 million to his 2025 inaugural fund. The company initially sought to keep its ballroom contribution anonymous, according to documents obtained by The Washington Post. Its ballroom pledge was tied with Apple's for the largest disclosed corporate contribution, the Post reported.

Those ties are drawing renewed scrutiny from congressional Democrats, including Sen. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., who pressed Huang in November 2025 about Nvidia's ballroom donation and White House contacts. Blumenthal re-upped his warning on Thursday, while expected incoming House oversight Chairman Robert Garcia, D-Calif., vowed to investigate corporate corruption when Democrats take control in January.

Trump continued to defend Huang on Sunday, calling him an "honorable guy" and saying he had heard nothing suggesting wrongdoing. The president was briefed about the suspected Chinese intelligence operation in October 2025 but chose not to act, according to The New York Times. Secretary of State Marco Rubio oversaw a classified memo detailing the concerns this spring, the Times reported.

The president's defense underscores how Huang has become one of Trump's most prominent allies in the technology industry, repeatedly praising the president, attending White House events and accompanying him on a trip to China in May. Trump is due to return to China next week for a summit with other world leaders.

Congressional scrutiny on Huang ramped up after the Times reported Oct. 29 that U.S. intelligence determined Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen who is an executive at Singapore-based cloud computing company Megaspeed, was funneling information to Beijing and had “developed a close relationship” with Jensen Huang. The two Huangs are unrelated.

House Republican committee leaders on Thursday asked Jensen Huang to sit for a closed-door briefing on his ties with Alice Huang, saying recent reports "raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information."

In a statement to his company on Sunday, Jensen Huang admitted to the affair and apologized. He also revealed that Nvidia's board of directors has "initiated an independent investigation," which he said he supports and will cooperate with.

"I love our country and our company. I am committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security. I am proud and grateful to be an American," the CEO said.

Nvidia didn't immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., told CBS News on Monday morning that "high-level" Trump administration officials would lead a classified briefing for lawmakers later in the day. An administration official, speaking on condition of anonymity to discuss the private meeting, told CNBC U.S. intelligence chiefs John Ratcliffe and Jay Clayton were previously scheduled to lead a classified briefing on threats related to Iran with top congressional leaders.

Later Monday morning, the Times reported that the Commerce Department was investigating Megaspeed until August 2025, when the probe was abruptly transferred to the Department of Justice following outreach from Nvidia's general counsel about the matter.

Thomas Wackman, spokesman for Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security, told CNBC in a statement, "BIS conducts all enforcement strictly by the book. BIS enforces aggressively across the board, including with respect to chip smuggling, and enforcement is at an all-time high." The DOJ did not respond to CNBC's request for comment.

"NVIDIA's CEO just confirmed a relationship with a suspected Chinese spy," Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee who is expected to chair the panel next year, posted on X Monday morning. "Congress is back today. We have legislation ready to go to stop advanced AI chips from fueling China's military."

Huang's access to Trump has coincided with a series of policy reversals that eased restrictions on Nvidia's chip sales to China, a lucrative market the company has fought to preserve.

In April 2025, Huang attended a $1 million-per-person dinner at Mar-a-Lago, after which the administration briefly held off on restricting Nvidia's H20 chips before imposing the limits days later. Following another meeting with Trump in July, the administration reversed course and began approving sales to China.

In December 2025, days after another meeting with Huang, Trump announced plans to permit exports of Nvidia's more powerful H200 chips to approved Chinese customers. The Commerce Department formally relaxed its licensing restrictions the following month.

Huang kept up the pressure in 2026, accompanying Trump on a trip to China as Nvidia fought congressional efforts to tighten export controls.

But Nvidia's success in Washington has yet to translate into unfettered access to China. Beijing has withheld approval for large-scale H200 imports, leaving hundreds of thousands of chips awaiting shipment as of early November.

Nvidia has also quietly ramped up its Washington influence operation, spending a record $2.15 million on federal lobbying in the third quarter — up 72% from the previous quarter and 10% from a year earlier, according to company disclosures. Its $4.69 million in 2026 lobbying expenses through September nearly matches the $4.97 million it spent in all of 2025.

The company's in-house lobbyists include alumni of congressional offices from key Republicans and Democrats, including Rep. Michael McCaul, R-Texas, who previously chaired the House Foreign Affairs Committee.

In June, Nvidia hired former Obama Commerce Department official and Intel lobbying chief Bruce Andrews to lead its Washington operation, then launched its first employee-funded PAC in August. The PAC raised more than $263,000 through September but had yet to back any candidates, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission filings. Its next disclosure is due Dec. 3. Separately, two Nvidia executives — Gavin Sherry, vice president of software, and Sundeep Madra, vice president of hardware — each contributed $443,000 to the Republican National Committee on June 24.

The donations help illustrate the depth of Huang and Trump's relationship as the $4.3 trillion tech behemoth Nvidia buoys the U.S. economy and lends crucial advantage over China in the race for AI dominance.

They also raise further questions about the Trump administration's response to U.S. intelligence reportedly revealing that the loyal and powerful Nvidia CEO had a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

"Obviously Nvidia needs to take significant steps in its operations, security, and policy advocacy to ensure it takes national security concerns more seriously," Chris McGuire, a senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council on Foreign Relations, said in an X post Monday. "But it is even more important that the Department of Commerce tighten up export controls to prevent things like this from ever happening again.

"It is utterly baffling that despite being privy to this information for months, they have not taken even the most basic actions to protect the country, such as cutting off a company that is literally run by Chinese spies from buying AI chips," McGuire wrote.

#2903. Giannis Antetokounmpo says he's 'speechless' over criticism from former coach Doc Rivers: 'That's insane'

Miami Heat star Giannis Antetokounmpo was "speechless" over criticism from his former Milwaukee Bucks coach Doc Rivers.

Rivers made those comments during an appearance on "The Bill Simmons Podcast," which was posted Thursday. During the episode, Rivers said Antetokounmpo wanted to leave the Bucks but wouldn't ask for a trade so he could continue being viewed as the good guy.

Rivers later added that Antetokounmpo "doesn't necessarily have this super IQ" and that he "really has one way: Downhill, force, force."

When asked about those comments Sunday, Antetokounmpo initially said he was "speechless" before trying to brush them aside, per ESPN.

"First of all, he's not my coach," Antetokounmpo told reporters after Miami's 114-108 victory over the Sacramento Kings. "My coach is Coach Spo [Erik Spoelstra], but [Rivers] is a guy — a coach — that I've worked with in the past. I'm speechless."

"You got to go ask him why he thought that," Antetokounmpo said. "That's on him, that's not on me. I just got to focus on my team and what I got to do and how I can be better and how can I help the team."

Later on, however, Antetokounmpo implied Rivers was fueled by "bitterness and pettiness."

"But God, that's crazy. Never let bitterness or pettiness get in front of your goals and dreams. For every athlete out there, never let that — that's insane. But you got to go ask him why he said what he said. Not me."

Heat coach Erik Spoelstra was also asked about Rivers' comments. The Heat coach declined to feed into them, saying he liked and respected Rivers.

Rivers and Antetokounmpo worked together for two-and-a-half years in Milwaukee. While the team made the playoffs in two of those seasons, it disappointed, falling in the first round in both years.

Antetokounmpo performed extremely well in his first two years under Rivers, averaging 30.4 points per game. The star big man spent a large stretch of last season injured.

The Bucks responded to the down year by jettisoning both men. Rivers stepped down from his role as head coach and Antetokounmpo was traded to the Heat. Rivers opted to return to the broadcast booth for the 2026-27 NBA season. Antetokounmpo is averaging 27.5 points and 12.8 rebounds in his first season with the Heat.

Carter turned down college scholarships to power programs like Indiana, Mississippi, Pittsburgh, Rutgers, West Virginia and Wisconsin to start his college football career at Toledo, where he posted a 5-5 record before transferring to South Alabama for his final two seasons of eligibility. #2901. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: Wall Street dips in again. What needs to go right

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

China has long wanted global investors' money to bolster the prominence of its domestic stock market. Giving foreigners that access hasn't happened overnight. Why are Wall Street firms still trying?

Global investment firms are eyeing mainland China not just for its tech stocks, but for the millions of affluent people who could become their clients.

Mainland Chinese with more than $125,000 in bankable assets hold a combined $27 trillion in wealth, according to financial analytics firm Crisil Coalition Greenwich. And the millionaire population has surged — to nearly 4 million, up from 1 million a decade ago.

On the heels of getting Beijing's approval to establish a wholly-owned securities company, Citi's CEO Jane Fraser is kicking off the U.S. bank's annual China conference this week in Shanghai. It's the latest U.S. financial firm to benefit from China's pledge after the Trump-Xi summit in September to approve applications from all foreign financial institutions to operate in the country.

Also headlining Citi's China conference in person is Anna Wintour, former editor-in-chief of Vogue. Condé Nast did not respond to a request for comment.

"The environment is meaningfully more open than it was a decade ago, although it remains highly regulated," said Nuno Cordeiro, head of wealth management, and Fabio Zheng, senior product manager, wealth management, at Crisil Coalition Greenwich.

They pointed out that compared to past years, the property market slowdown has also created greater demand for diversification and advisory services.

"The question today is no longer whether foreign firms can participate. It is whether they can generate sufficient scale and returns within those constraints," they said in an email. "Consequently, most successful strategies combine greater local presence with partnerships that provide access to domestic distribution."

Franklin Templeton, which oversees $1.8 trillion in assets, and ChinaAMC, which manages over 3.1 trillion yuan ($462.5 billion), signed a strategic partnership on Oct. 28 for sharing market insights and jointly developing products.

They are already working on co-branded ETFs across Hong Kong, U.S. and European fund structures.

"Just as a lot of international investors [are] interested in China investment, Chinese investors are looking for global investment vehicles," Sherrie Dai, managing director, global capital, at ChinaAMC, told me.

A Hong Kong-listed ETF launched with Franklin Templeton on Sept. 29 holds around 40% U.S. stocks and 60% Hong Kong-listed ones.

Such products could, in six to 12 months, meet Beijing's threshold for letting mainland China-based investors participate, Dai said. Her company is also launching products for investors in Brazil to allocate to Chinese tech companies.

Since early 2025, DeepSeek's AI breakthroughs and other Chinese tech advances have gained foreign investors' attention. U.S.-based Roundhill Investments included China stocks in its thematic ETFs focusing on humanoids, memory chips or other tech — but exposure to mainland-listed names may sometimes come through derivatives.

Performance hasn't helped. Chinese stocks have tumbled this year, significantly underperforming their U.S. peers.

On the China business front, foreign financial firms have also struggled. In late 2025, BlackRock decided to shut down its three-year-old China Innovation Fund, which only had $31.7 million in net assets. Vanguard in 2023 sold its stake in a mainland China mutual fund operator to majority owner Ant Group.

Peter Alexander, founder and managing director of Z-Ben Advisors in Shanghai, attributed part of the challenge to "micromanagement" from headquarters outside China.

"Global groups have failed in China due solely to their inability to make the necessary adjustments to how it is they operate to best fit the Chinese market," he said.

Capital controls, a state-dominated system and varying levels of investor communication contribute to a mainland Chinese stock market quite different from that of the U.S.

State-linked funds, known as the "National Team," often step up stock purchases to limit market sell-offs.

Last week, amid reports of a pause in new Chinese IPOs, a senior financial regulator warned in a speech that too much capital concentration in tech can drive up leverage, while crowding out traditional industries.

Such headlines can support short-term stock market gains. But for longer-term investors, who might not be able to buy in immediately, rapid price surges raise the risk that they are "buying at the top," said Liqian Ren, a quantitative manager at U.S.-based fund manager WisdomTree.

High volatility means those investors can only hold a certain amount of Chinese stocks due to perceived risk, she said. The market "becomes a trading vehicle. That's not necessarily good for China."

"Ultimately you need the market to figure it out. You need the punishment to come from the market."

China expands overseas mining stakes, tightening grip on critical minerals

Chinese mineral acquisitions abroad in the first three quarters of the year along marked the highest annually since at least 2018, according to Rhodium Group. That adds to the existing rare earths leverage Beijing has used in U.S. trade tensions. China is also demanding lower tariffs before buying more U.S. agricultural goods.

What the Democratic midterms sweep changes for U.S.-China relations

Democrats taking power from Republicans in Congress raises risks for U.S.-China tensions around Taiwan, while hardening tech competition, analysts said. Russia and China may feel the need to test the U.S. if they feel Trump is on "his way out," according to Robert Kagan, senior fellow at the Brookings Institution.

Moonshot turned to Europe for Nvidia Blackwell access amid U.S. China curbs, sources tell CNBC

Deals have been discussed this year for Moonshot to rent capacity from neoclouds hosting Nvidia graphics processing units, or GPUs, at facilities in Europe, four people familiar with the matter said. Meanwhile in Asia, reports of a prison sentencing in South Korea reflect the growing AI chip talent war.

Nov. 10 - 13: Citi China Conference in Shanghai and Shenzhen

Nov. 13: Extended Singles Day shopping festival wraps up

Nov. 16: Retail sales, industrial production and fixed-asset investment

#2899. Nvidia's CEO turmoil gives newly empowered Democrats fresh ammunition in pursuit of oversight

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's close ties to the U.S. government were already poised to loosen with Democrats winning control of Congress and promising far more oversight of AI development than what the chip giant has experienced under Republican leadership.

That was before he admitted on Sunday to having an extramarital affair.

The CEO was romantically involved with Alice Huang, according to The Financial Times, who U.S. intelligence officials believe is a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, The New York Times reported. Alice Huang is also affiliated with an Nvidia customer called Megaspeed that's been accused of helping restricted Chinese companies acquire Nvidia's artificial intelligence chips.

Huang apologized to staffers in a note on Sunday for being "unfaithful to my wife," and said the company's board was investigating any related national security concerns. Nvidia's board said it has "confidence in Jensen," who co-founded the company in 1993 and has led it ever since, building a net worth of more than $150 billion in the process.

Even before last week's midterm elections, which saw Democrats win sweeping majorities in the House and Senate, vocal party leaders wanted answers from Huang about China's access to advanced Nvidia chips and his company's lobbying for favorable legislation. Sen. Elizabeth Warren of Massachusetts said days before the election, when the spy story was first surfacing, that "we must pass bipartisan legislation to keep our advanced AI technology out of China's hands." Months before that, in June, Huang declined an invitation from Warren to testify before the Senate Banking Committee.

Democratic House leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York, who is the presumed speaker of the House in the new Congress, told CBS News on Monday that it is "a significant issue of concern."

Jeffries said there is a classified briefing with White House officials later Monday, and he expects the Nvidia CEO's affair to be discussed. He also said he had not seen the memo from Secretary of State Marco Rubio about Jensen Huang's relationship with the alleged spy, which was reported by The New York Times.

With Democrats about to take the gavel, Delaware Sen. Chris Coons, who is in line to chair the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, wrote on X on Saturday that "Congress will investigate and get answers," following the allegations that appeared in a New York Times report Friday.

It's a sharp reversal for Huang, who gained first-name status with President Donald Trump and has been closely allied with the administration in trying to limit AI regulation. Nvidia shares plunged 12% in the two days after the election, pacing a drop in AI-related stocks, on fears that incoming lawmakers, at the federal and state levels, and a host of newly passed ballot measures will slow the buildout and halt the AI boom.

Huang is a singular voice in the AI industry, as his company's technology is used to train nearly all major AI models. He's also turned Nvidia into an essential source of financing for AI startups, and pulled together some of the biggest names on Wall Street into a group that announced plans in August to raise $500 billion for investing in the buildout.

At Nvidia, Huang is the sole public face of the company and has no clear second-in-command. He decides which projects are a strategic priority, and frequently sends short emails to individual engineers about details. Huang has said he has 60 direct reports and reviews every employee's salary. Meanwhile, he packs his calendar with meetings with world leaders and CEOs, alongside keynote speeches at conferences and trade shows.

"Nvidia is not an ordinary company and Jensen is not an ordinary CEO; he IS Nvidia," Bernstein analyst Stacy Rasgon wrote in a note on Sunday, adding that it's not clear who would succeed him.

Patrick Moorhead, CEO at Moor Insights & Strategy, said in an email that "Jensen holding so much power is why Nvidia moves faster than anyone."

Unlike many of his tech peers, Huang is not chairman of the board. Rather, he's one of 11 board members, and there's no designated chair.

One investor close to Huang told CNBC that, depending on what the board's investigation finds, he could foresee Huang taking a leave of absence.

"Today's news will not slow Nvidia," a company spokesperson said in a statement. "Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption."

But scrutiny of Nvidia is set to intensify on Wall Street and in Washington, where Huang's level of influence is almost certain to change.

He has been a major advocate for more chip exports to China, arguing that it's better for AI labs there to build on American technology than to encourage the development of domestic alternatives. Huang joined Trump on Air Force One in May on a China trip that's now raised additional questions about what the administration and Huang knew about Chinese spying at the time.

One question Democrats are likely to probe is whether the Trump administration was aware of Huang's relationship with the alleged Chinese spy, and whether the Nvidia CEO influenced American policy, said Sam Bresnick, a research fellow at the Center for Security and Emerging Technology.

"That is going to be very high up on the Democrats' lists of people to investigate to try to figure out to what extent was the Trump administration aware of what was going on," Bresnick said.

But pressure isn't just coming from Democrats. House Republican leaders have already asked Huang for a closed-door briefing on Chinese spying issues later this month.

Trump has continued to back Huang, telling reporters on Sunday that he's an "honorable guy" and has "done an amazing job for our country."

"I think he probably has an excuse for it," Trump said of the incident.

Amid all the noise about the election results and the spying revelations, Nvidia is facing a crucial stretch on the markets.

Neoclouds CoreWeave and Nebius, which are both backed by Nvidia and are big buyers of its chips, are scheduled to report earnings this week after their stocks lost roughly half their value over the past month. Investors are particularly concerned about their hefty debt loads and how they'll continue to fund their buildouts with interest rates rising and lenders turning skittish.

Nvidia has its own earnings report coming on Nov. 17, when Huang, assuming he sticks with the typical format, will face questions from analysts. And then there's the Anthropic initial public offering, which is critical for Nvidia as it's both an investor in the Claude creator and counts the AI lab as a significant customer.

A couple of weeks after earnings, Nvidia is hosting a large conference in Washington, D.C., where Huang is scheduled to give the keynote on Dec. 1.

Bullish investors and analysts can still point to the insatiable demand for Nvidia's technology. The company recently projected 70% sales growth in its upcoming fiscal year. Analysts polled by FactSet expect nearly $694 billion in fiscal 2028 sales.

"We believe demand for the company's products seems unlikely to be affected," Rasgon wrote. "But it seems plausible to postulate increased risks (regulatory etc.) as AI buildouts become an increasingly political issue."

— CNBC's Seema Mody, Katie Tarasov, Ashley Capoot and MacKenzie Sigalos contributed reporting to this report.

#2897. Trump questions midterm election results, revives voting-machine conspiracy

President Donald Trump on Monday questioned the legitimacy of the midterm election results, resurrecting his pattern of raising doubts about electoral losses for himself and other Republicans.

Trump's social media post raising questions about the election came six days after his fellow Republicans suffered historic defeats at the polls nationwide, including in several GOP strongholds like Texas and South Carolina.

"Does anybody really believe that this 'Election' was honest," Trump wrote on Truth Social.

"How could the Polls be so wrong??? Some early voting 'dumps' were so large that it was impossible to win, no matter how many people went to vote," the president said. "It's all about the machines, which are far more important than the voter!"

Trump's post was a sharp pivot from what has been a conciliatory tone after last Tuesday's thumping of Republican candidates, which resulted in Democrats regaining majorities in both the Senate and House of Representatives.

On Election Night, Trump told The New York Times that he was "absolutely willing" to accept the results of the midterms, and that he was willing to work with Democrats.

"We'll, you know, look forward to working with them," Trump told The Times. "We'll probably work something out."

The next day, Trump said, "I'll get along probably with the Democrats unless they want to be destructive."

Later in the week, he announced he was replacing White House Chief of Staff Susie Wiles with her deputy, Stephen Miller, the architect of the administration's aggressive immigration enforcement crackdown, which has drawn widespread criticism from Democrats.

Sen. Chuck Schumer, a New York Democrat who could be the majority leader in the Senate in January, said in a post on X, "Donald Trump's pathetic return to election denialism was inevitable."

"He can whine all he wants, but nothing will stop this inescapable truth: For the last two years of his presidency, Trump will face the largest Democratic congressional majorities in decades, all because he has been an unprecedented failure," Schumer said.

After his electoral loss to former President Joe Biden in 2020, Trump argued, without evidence, that he only lost as a result of widespread ballot fraud.

Trump's claims, which were joined by dozens of unsuccessful court cases challenging election procedures, culminated in the Jan. 6, 2021, attack on the U.S. Capitol by a mob of his supporters after he spoke at a rally outside the White House.

The riot disrupted a joint session of Congress for hours. Later that day, Congress confirmed Biden's election by the Electoral College.

Critics of Trump's conspiracy theories about elections have noted that he lost the 2020 race when he was president, but won the 2024 presidential election, when Biden was in the White House.

They also noted that in the past week, Republicans had control of states that saw major Democratic wins that few analysts would have predicted a year ago, including Texas, Kansas, South Carolina, and Iowa.

#2894. 3 unexpected reasons the October jobs report was so strong

A little-noticed category in Friday's blockbuster jobs report is challenging the consensus view about job losses caused by artificial intelligence.

At least for now, the data suggests the U.S. is gaining jobs as a result of the AI boom, not losing them.

And another category suggests the strong jobs numbers could have some staying power.

Nonfarm payrolls grew by 165,000 jobs in October, according to the Friday report from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. It was a surprising surge in hiring that blew way past Wall Street's expectations. Economists polled by Dow Jones had forecast growth of just 59,000 for the month.

Here are three surprising things you might have missed from the report.

Hiring for consultants surged in the month, sending the profession's total head count to a fresh record.

The labor force for management, scientific and technical consultants expanded by 17,300 jobs between September and October, the largest month-over-month increase in nearly two years. This category is a subset of the professional and business services sector tracked by BLS and accounted for 25% of the entire sector's gains for the month.

What's more, this workforce has grown by more than 45,000 jobs over the past year. That gain pushed the total employee base for these types of workers past 1.9 million for the first time on record, according to BLS data.

Hiring in the industry had largely stalled since 2023 following years of rapid expansion during the pandemic. Wall Street has questioned if the rise of artificial intelligence will curb demand for these services.

Those fears have led shares of consulting firm Gartner to lose more than half of their value over the past three years. Accenture and FTI Consulting have dropped about 18% and 9% over that period, even as the S&P 500 has climbed roughly 74%.

Now, some economists are speculating that the recent jump could be driven by businesses hiring consultants to help them adopt AI. Gartner forecast in September that worldwide AI spending would total $2.7 trillion in 2026, a nearly 50% increase year over year.

"The AI buildout and corporate interests to increase utilization will keep the technical consultants employed," said Jeffrey Roach, chief economist at LPL Financial.

Roach called the consulting sector a bright spot in October that is "worth watching." He noted that these jobs also saw double-digit growth in the late 1990s, as firms raced to implement new internet technology into their businesses.

So far, data suggests that AI may be adding more jobs to the labor market than it is taking away through automation. An assessment by Moody's Analytics found that AI has created a net 786,000 jobs since ChatGPT launched in November 2022, driven in part by data center construction.

"Wasn't AI supposed to destroy jobs? The most dystopian scenarios even called for mass unemployment," Mark Zandi, Moody's chief economist, wrote in a social media post on Monday. "That risk hasn't gone away, and AI-driven productivity gains could still shift into a higher gear. But for now, AI is adding to job growth, not subtracting from it."

Hiring of temporary workers also drove growth, which could signal that the labor market is heating up.

The subsector's payrolls grew by 18,600 in October, one of its largest month-over-month increases since 2022. This category is another part of the professional and business services sector and accounted for 27% of the sector's gains for the month.

The total size of this workforce is still well below record highs set in 2022. But headcount has grown roughly 3% since December and now sits at levels last seen in April 2025, the month that President Donald Trump's rollout of his plan for steep tariffs put corporate America on alert.

Economists see temporary hiring as a leading indicator in the labor market. Conventional wisdom suggests that companies that hire new workers on a short-term basis often transition them to full-time roles later.

That could be what's happening here, but the fact that the end of the year is approaching makes it harder to tell. The boost could be a result of hiring as the holiday shopping season approaches — and if employers are staffing up for the holidays, they are less likely to retain those workers when January rolls around. Such hiring hasn't typically happened this early, but the season creeps earlier each year. The BLS adjusts its numbers to account for seasonal trends, but won't immediately make an adjustment if the trend is changing.

One hint that holiday hiring may be shifting comes in a separate category of workers: couriers and messengers. This is another area that acts as a barometer for holiday labor demand, and just over one out of every 10 new jobs in the month was in this category.

That category "bears watching for reversal in the months ahead," said Jason Pride, chief of investment strategy and research at Glenmede, a wealth manager.

Hiring related to political campaigns in the final weeks of an expensive election cycle could have also boosted payrolls.

Employment in political organizations has historically spiked heading into midterm or presidential elections, according to a BLS analysis. Staffing levels tend to peak in October of those years, the data shows. Nonfarm payrolls expanded in the months before the 2018 and 2022 midterm elections.

But the vast size of campaign coffers this cycle may have translated into more jobs than before.

The 2026 congressional election is slated to go down in history as the most expensive midterm on record, according to an analysis from OpenSecrets, a money-in-politics watchdog group. Billions of dollars spent on campaigns may have translated into paid gigs that involve door knocking, phone banking or other election-related work.

#2893. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2892. Bengals Week 9 awards in Spain victory vs. Falcons

The Cincinnati Bengals went to Spain and came home with their first-ever win outside the United States, a 34-17 handling of the Atlanta Falcons at the Bernabéu. It was their fourth straight win, it moved them to 6-2, and it put them in a tie with Pittsburgh atop the AFC North heading into next Sunday night's showdown at Paycor.

Before we get to the Steelers, let's hand out some hardware. Here are my nominees for Offensive MVP, Defensive MVP, and Unsung Hero. Then you tell me who takes home the trophy in the comments section.

Joe Burrow, QB. Burrow went 24-of-29 for 271 yards, three touchdowns, and no turnovers, good for a 140.1 passer rating. He also scored on a 1-yard sneak, giving him four total touchdowns. He was nearly flawless in the first half, going 16-of-18 for 234 yards and two scores, as the Bengals built a 24-10 halftime lead.

His 21 touchdown passes lead the NFL, and he has 16 total touchdowns over the last four games. One week after throwing three interceptions against Tennessee, he played a clean, efficient game. If the MVP race were decided today, he'd have a strong case.

Ja'Marr Chase, WR. Atlanta scored on its opening drive, and Chase answered on the Bengals' first possession by hauling in a 47-yard touchdown from Burrow to tie it at 7. He was already at eight catches for 142 yards by halftime, the most receiving yards he has ever had in a first half.

He finished with 10 catches for 155 yards on 11 targets, and his seven receiving touchdowns lead the AFC. Zac Taylor said it best after the game: "That's just Ja'Marr Chase. Best in the world."

Tee Higgins, WR. Five catches for 32 yards won't jump off the page, but Higgins was the finisher. He caught touchdowns from 4 and 8 yards out, giving him four touchdown catches in the last two games and six on the season. When the Bengals got into the red zone, Burrow looked for No. 5.

Barrett Carter, LB. Carter tied for the team lead with 10 tackles and broke up a pass. Then, with the Bengals up two scores in the fourth quarter, he picked off Michael Penix Jr. at the Atlanta 43 and effectively ended any thought of a comeback. It was his second career interception and his first of 2026.

It's been an up-and-down year for the linebackers in coverage, so seeing Carter make the game-sealing play was a welcome sight.

Bryan Cook, S. Cook also led the team with 10 tackles, five of them solo. On a day when Bijan Robinson and the Falcons' run game kept getting to the second level, Cook was the guy cleaning things up. Without him, a few of those 10-yard gains would become 30-yard gains.

Demetrius Knight Jr., LB. Knight added eight tackles, five solo, and was a steady presence in the middle of a defense that held Atlanta to seven points after the opening quarter.

Josh Newton, CB. Newton had five tackles, and the biggest play he made doesn't show up in the box score as anything more than a pass breakup. Atlanta dialed up a flea-flicker shot into the end zone, and Newton was right there to knock it away. In a game that was 24-17 in the third quarter, that's a big swing.

Dohnte Meyers, WR/KR. Meyers was all over the box score. He had two catches for 47 yards, including a 42-yarder, a 16-yard run, and four kickoff returns for 100 yards. That's 163 all-purpose yards from a guy most fans weren't talking about before Sunday. When the Bengals needed a spark outside of Chase and Higgins, Meyers gave them one.

Evan McPherson, K. McPherson drilled a 57-yarder just before halftime to make it 24-10, then added a 43-yarder in the fourth. He's now made 23 straight field goals, the longest streak in franchise history and the longest active streak in the NFL. We don't talk enough about how automatic he's been.

Samaje Perine, RB. Perine handled the dirty work with 11 carries for 43 yards. He also closed the game out the smart way: after picking up a late first down, he slid down instead of chasing extra yards, so the Bengals could run out the clock. That's veteran football.

The offensive line, led by Dalton Risner. Risner made his 100th career regular-season start, and the line paved the way for 123 rushing yards at 5.1 yards a carry. It also allowed just two sacks. After weeks of a stuck run game, the line finally won some battles up front.

Those are my nominees. Who gets your Offensive MVP: Burrow or Chase? Is Carter's game-sealing pick enough for Defensive MVP? And who's your Unsung Hero from Madrid?

#2891. The second half surge

Welcome to our series grading every member of the San Diego Padres' 40-man roster. The roster being evaluated is current as of Oct. 18, prior to the end of the World Series. Because of that, it will include players who will be free agents once the postseason concludes. Each player will be graded on their performance in the 2026 season, including the playoffs. That performance will be weighted based on how much time the player spent with the team (availability is the best ability).

When Jackson Merrill trotted out to center field on Opening Day of the 2024 season, he had played just six games at the position. Merrill was drafted by San Diego as a lifetime shortstop, but transitioned to center field and flourished. In his rookie year, Merrill finished runner-up in NL Rookie of the Year voting behind Pittsburgh Pirates ace Paul Skenes. He had a bit of a slump in his sophomore season, being held back by injury.

However, 2026 was a return to form. Merrill recorded the first 20/20 season of his young career, finishing the year with a career-high 26 homers and 27 stolen bases. If not for a slightly unproductive first half of the season, Merrill might have recorded the first 30/30 season in Padres' franchise history. Along with Fernando Tatis Jr., Merrill buoyed the Padres' second-half surge.

Merrill's overall metrics have consistently declined since his rookie year. The center fielder's batting average, on-base percentage, slugging percentage and OPS have all decreased each year. Still, 2026 was his most productive season yet with a career-high in combination of runs scored and runs batted in (173).

There's a lot to suggest that Merrill was unlucky. His .270 expected batting average was 26 points higher than his actual mark (.254) while his .491 xSLG was 51 points above his actual (.440). Much of that may have to do with Merrill's unbelievably high strikeout rates (career-high 160 strikeouts). If he can mitigate the Ks in 2027, Merrill could be phenomenal leader in the San Diego clubhouse this coming season.

#2890. The story behind Mike Conley’s first Celtics start: “I thought they were joking”

MILWAUKEE — Mike Conley didn't hear from his coach that he was starting in Sunday's game.

He heard it from the very Celtics teammates who picked him.

The 39-year-old point guard, in his 20th NBA season, got his first start of the year against the Milwaukee Bucks after Joe Mazzulla handed the decision to Jayson Tatum and Derrick White, Conley exclusively told CelticsBlog late Sunday night outside the visiting locker room.

The two Celtics veterans decided that Conley, who had barely played through Boston's first 9 games of the season, had earned the opportunity with his strong fourth-quarter play against the Charlotte Hornets.

"I heard it from D-White and JT," Conley said after the team's 117-97 win over the Bucks. "They were like, 'Hey, you're starting in Milwaukee.' I was like, 'What?'"

Prior to Sunday, Conley had played a total of 22 minutes all season.

So when Tatum and White approached him after Boston's blowout win over the Hornets on Friday, he didn't take them seriously.

"They looked at me after the Charlotte game. They're like, 'So you want to start next game?'" Conley said. "I'm just like, 'What are you talking about?' It's not really on my radar at the moment, so I thought they were joking, honestly."

Conley had played well in his fourth-quarter stint against the Hornets, and Mazzulla and the team appreciated his ball pressure.

"I thought Mike did a great job getting us off to a [good] start in the fourth quarter against Charlotte with his ball pressure, with his defensive activity for us," Mazzulla said. "And I wanted that same thing to start the game."

But Mazzulla, who has openly stressed player empowerment all season long, wanted to give the players a chance to decide.

On Sunday morning, Mazzulla walked into the Celtics' team meeting and asked the room who should start; Jordan Walsh started in 6 of the Celtics' first 9 games, while Hugo Gonzalez got the chance in three of them.

"He was like, 'So who's starting?'" Conley recalled. "He left it up to his players. And JT is like, 'Mike, me…,' and gave the starting lineup. I'm like, it's real."

That meant Conley, White, Tatum, Paul George, and Neemias Queta started against the Bucks.

Conley said he wasn't nervous — he's started for most of his career, after all —but it was his first time sharing the floor with Tatum and George, which he thoroughly enjoyed.

"It was the first time literally stepping on the court with them," he said. "It was a lot of fun, just to get out there and get some run and play in a competitive atmosphere."

The Celtics didn't get off to a great start on Sunday night; Bucks forward Kyle Kuzma opened 6-of-10 for 16 first-quarter points, and the Celtics trailed by as many as 14 in the opening frame.

But Mazzulla didn't attribute the slow start to Conley's play.

"I thought his minutes were great," Mazzulla said, adding that the Celtics missed several layups that helped put them in the hole.

Despite the slow start, the Celtics responded with a 33-18 second quarter, headlined by 16 points from a scorching-hot Paul George. They ultimately pulled away for a 117-97 win, improving to an 8-2 record on the season.

Postgame, Mazzulla downplayed the importance of who starts, as he has throughout much of his tenure as head coach.

"At the end of the day, the starting lineup is one piece of the 15 to 20 lineups that we play in the game," Mazzulla said. "All the lineups matter."

In 20 seasons in the NBA, Conley said, he had never seen players get a say in who starts or take on the kinds of off-court responsibilities that Celtics players have this season.

"This is the first place," he said. "Our players do scouts. We do film sessions. We have so much empowerment from Joe, and I think it's just a unique atmosphere and culture built around that. So, that's just another extension of it."

Conley said the team has handled the constantly changing lineups well because everyone keeps the team's success front and center.

"Everybody, Jordan Walsh to Hugo, whoever started prior games, nobody cares," he said. "Everybody just goes out there and supports each other, and that's cool."

While all the players have had the chance to take on more significant responsibilities in film and scouting, perhaps no player has taken on more than Conley.

"I've got a lot to give, with my experience and things I've been through, and how I see and read the game maybe differently than some other players," Conley said. "So I try to give that to as many guys as I can, and try to prepare them and give them options, and have them see things through my eyes a couple times throughout the games. And to see them go out there and execute is pretty cool. And I know Joe supports me in that, so he gives me the freedom to pull a guy to the side, or tell him to do this or that, or change something up, or even tell Joe to do something different. So it's a unique, unique spot, and then also being able to play too — it's pretty cool."

During training camp, Conley told CelticsBlog that he knew his on-court role would be inconsistent, and that some nights he'd play and some nights he wouldn't.

"It's part of the job at this stage of my career," he told CelticsBlog at training camp in Charleston. "I understand it. I understand there's going to be a lot of times where I don't play. There's going to be some times where I'm needed. There are other times where I'm just needed to be the voice for a big stretch of the season. Being comfortable with that, and being able to own that, is my role and my job."

What has surprised him, however, is that the Stay-Ready bench role has actually made him expend more energy, not less.

"You're not just relaxing and chilling," he said. "You're actually working harder than you probably ever have, because you never know when your number is going to be called. Your wheels have to be turning, your brain has to be on."

Mazzulla said he's been impressed with Conley's minutes on the court.

"There's been times where he just comes in off the bench, he gets a shot right away," he said. "His defensive presence has been great. So I think that's just as important, because he still takes pride in playing. That's great to see at this stage of his career."

Still, Conley's off-court impact might be his most profound contribution to the team. On Sunday, Mazzulla called Conley "an extension of the coaching staff", and said he's made a concerted effort to learn from him.

"He has 20 years of experience, and I'm going to pick his brain just as much as the players are," Mazzulla said. "He's played for great coaches, had great teammates. We all just need to be able to take from his wisdom."

Conley has run film sessions and handled part of the scouting and defensive game-planning for the Celtics' game against the Orlando Magic last week, in which they held the Magic to just 97 points. When he signed in Boston, he knew he'd be expected to help lead in the locker room.

"I knew that I'd obviously be a voice and a vet leader and all that stuff," Conley said. "But to the extent of running film sessions, picking out clips, stuff like that — I didn't know that was going to be part of the job description."

"It's something I think is unique and really cool about being here," he said. "And, I welcome it the rest of the year."

Asked whether this season's unique experience has pointed toward coaching, Conley laughed.

"I always tell them, I don't know if I can coach the young guys, man. Y'all run me in circles," he said. "But I'll never shut the door on it."

For now, he's enjoying the unique opportunity to be a key mind on one of the NBA's top teams and a rare active player who can take on real coaching responsibilities.

"Hopefully I continue to get better at it," Conley said, "and continue to bridge that gap of player and coach and teammate."

#2888. A key report for where inflation stood in October comes out Tuesday. Here's what to expect

Falling gasoline prices provided some relief to consumers in October, but Federal Reserve officials are increasingly concerned that inflation pressures elsewhere in the economy are proving harder to contain.

Prices at the pump declined by more than 4%, according to the Energy Information Administration, providing a respite from a surge that had added more than a dollar a gallon over the past year.

However, the consumer price index for the month, which the Bureau of Labor Statistics will release Tuesday at 8:30 a.m., is expected to show only a slight let-up.

Meanwhile, Fed officials say they are worried that inflation initially driven higher by President Donald Trump's tariffs and the Iran war is proving more persistent than expected, with price pressures emerging in sectors beyond those directly affected by the shocks.

At a time when the central bank is increasingly confident about the state of the labor market, concerns over the cost of living are what are top of mind for the Fed.

"We've been moving up to what historically we'd consider full employment, and the big problem has all been on the inflation side. We go around, we talk to people everywhere in this district, all we hear about is affordability on the business side, the cost," Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee said Monday during an event in his home district. "So we've got to come to terms with this cost-side and inflation-side failure."

The CPI reading for all items is expected to show an increase of 0.3%. Core inflation, which excludes volatile food and energy costs, is forecast to increase 0.2%, according to the Dow Jones consensus forecast. While both would be slower than the respective September monthly gains of 0.6% and 0.3%, they still represent further price pressures.

There will be no annual reading for October because of the government shutdown a year ago, which prevented the BLS from collecting some data and compiling a full index level. Because of the data issue, there isn't a comparable figure for the index.

Bank of America, using an assumed baseline interpolating data for the period last year, estimated the annual growth rates at 3.8% for headline and 2.7% for core, both 0.1 percentage point higher than September and well above the Fed's 2% goal.

Goolsbee said the current run on inflation is defying what normally could be expected from supply shocks and tariffs, with the current situation intensified by the hyperscaler build-out related to artificial intelligence.

"It was supposed to have gone away by now, and we're now starting to see the inflation kicking up in areas that probably weren't from oil prices and tariffs, like a bunch of services. Now this stuff with the AI data center build-out is, to my eyes, starting to look more just like old-fashioned demand overheating," he said.

The massive investment in data centers is creating demand for construction workers, electricity and equipment, potentially adding to price pressures even as AI promises longer-term productivity gains, he added.

Though the Fed in October voted to hold its benchmark interest rate in place, markets are pricing in a better than 80% probability that the central bank will raise rates by a quarter percentage point at its next meeting in December, according to CME FedWatch data.

Goolsbee, who doesn't vote on the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee this year but will in 2027, didn't commit to which way he was leaning on additional rate hikes, though he said current conditions could warrant action.

"The conventional wisdom there is: Be aggressive up front, to never let the economy overheat, because once it gets overheated, it's extremely painful to get rid of that kind of overheating," he said. "So I think we've got to watch out in that space."

The CPI release comes with economic growth strong — 3.4% in the third quarter for gross domestic product and tracking at 3.5% for the final three months of the year, according to the Atlanta Fed — and the labor market seemingly in solid shape as well. Nonfarm payrolls expanded by a much better-than-expected 165,000 in October, while the unemployment rate edged lower to 4.1%, though inflation-adjusted wage growth has been negative.

That puts the inflation reading squarely in focus.

Bank of America said it sees tariff impacts fading but that goods prices could be pushed higher because of AI demand. Further, the firm is cautious about "sticky" services prices as well as pressures from health insurance and autos.

The bank has been hawkish in terms of Fed expectations and sees a December hike as likely so long as there isn't an escalated stock market sell-off that might dissuade the central bank.

"This will be the final inflation report before the Fed's December 8-9 meeting. If our forecast is correct, we think it would effectively lock in another 25bp hike, all else equal," BofA economist Stephen Juneau said in a client note. "Absent an indisputably soft 0.1% [month-over-month] print, we doubt the inflation data will dissuade the Fed from hiking in December. The bigger risk to our call comes from financial markets."

#2887. Hegseth impeachment articles introduced by Rep. Massie

Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., on Monday introduced articles of impeachment against Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, triggering a vote in the House of Representatives as early as this week.

The unusual impeachment push against Hegseth by a member of his own party comes after Democrats swept to majorities in both the House and Senate during last week's midterm elections. Many Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, have cited the Iran war Hegseth has led as a reason for the electoral bludgeoning.

Massie, who lost his reelection bid in a Republican primary in May, introduced eight articles of impeachment against Hegseth. They include waging war in contravention of war powers resolutions Congress passed to limit war, extrajudicial killings tied to boat strikes in the Caribbean that purported to target drug smugglers and suppressing free speech by targeting Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., a veteran.

"Wherefore, Secretary of Defense Peter Hegseth, by such conduct, has demonstrated that he will remain a threat to the Constitution if allowed to remain in office, and has acted in a manner grossly incompatible with his duties and the rule of law," the articles, which Massie read from the House floor, read. "Peter Brian Hegseth thus warrants impeachment and trial, removal from office, and disqualification to hold and enjoy any office of honor, trust, or profit under the United States."

Massie's resolution will force House Republican leaders to put the measure up for a vote by the end of this week. Impeachment resolutions are privileged, meaning the House must act on them, table them or vote to refer them to committee within two legislative days.

President Donald Trump once again defended Hegseth later Monday, telling reporters at the White House he will stand by the secretary even if he is impeached.

"He won two wars. He was in charge," said Trump, who was impeached twice in his first presidential term.

Trump also credited Hegseth with boosting military recruitment, claiming there are "waiting lists of people trying to get in."

Congress has the authority to impeach Cabinet secretaries, though it is a highly unusual move. Republican leadership in the House has not yet laid out how they plan to deal with the Hegseth impeachment, and it is unclear whether they have the votes to dispose of Massie's measure following the election.

The last Cabinet Secretary to be impeached was Homeland Security Secretary Alejandro Mayorkas in 2024 over his handling of the U.S.-Mexico border. Before Mayorkas, a Cabinet secretary had not been impeached since Secretary of War William Belknap in 1876. Neither was convicted by the Senate.

If the House votes to impeach Hegseth, the Senate would be tasked with holding a trial and voting on removing the secretary from office. If convicted by a two-thirds majority of the Senate, Hegseth would be removed from office.

Republicans hold a razor-thin 218-214 majority in the House, meaning only a handful of Republican defections could result in Hegseth's impeachment.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., reiterated his support for impeaching Hegseth on Monday at his first press conference at the Capitol since last Tuesday's elections. "Pete Hegseth has abused his power, undermined America's national security, and he's the architect of the reckless and costly failed war of choice in Iran," Jeffries said.

Hegseth has appeared to be preparing for an impeachment fight. The secretary put out a video last week defending his actions atop the Pentagon, and the department has led a media campaign to "Stand. Fight. Defend the Republic."

Hegseth on Monday announced he would hold a press conference Tuesday morning at the Pentagon. His most recent press conference way May 5, just over two months after the Iran war began.

#2886. Trump says Jensen Huang told him Monday he didn't know paramour 'was a spy'

President Donald Trump said he spoke to Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Monday, and that Huang denied knowing that the woman he admitted having an extramarital affair with "was a spy" for China.

Trump told reporters at the White House that he was "amazed" by the allegations against Huang, which led Nvidia's board to initiate an independent investigation.

Trump suggested he had only recently learned of allegations that Megaspeed International executive Alice Huang was a Chinese spy.

"I didn't hear about it then, but a lot of people have heard about it, and Jensen totally denies that," Trump said.

"I asked him today because I did speak to him today just to see if he's feeling all right. 'How you doing?'" Trump said.

"And I asked him about that, and he ... said he had absolutely no idea that the person in question was a spy," the president said. "I asked him that question: 'Did you have any idea that she was a spy?' He said, 'I had no idea that she was a spy.' "

The New York Times on Oct. 29 reported that Alice Huang had been eyed by U.S. spy agencies "for possibly steering advanced Nvidia A.I. chips to China," and that "American officials had concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset, funneling information to Beijing."

The Times reported that about a year ago, Trump was briefed about the intelligence findings "and chose not to do anything with the information." The newspaper noted that Trump has been reluctant to disrupt chip sales by Nvdia, which are boosting the U.S. stock market.

The Times said the memo reported that Alice Huang was recruited to collect intelligence on the Nvidia CEO "as part of a drive by Beijing's top spy agency to learn more about American A.I. chips and potentially influence the company to push for its most advanced chips to be made available to China."

"I've been reading a little bit about it over a period of time, but not too long ago," Trump said Monday. "We'll look into it. I know the FBI is looking into it in terms of something else, and I think that he'll be in good shape."

Trump on Monday said of Jensen Huang, "He's suffering greatly."

"I believe he's suffering greatly. I read his statement, and I believe he's suffering greatly, more so because of his family, his wife, who he loves, and his family. But he is definitely suffering."

Asked if he would bring up the issue involving Huang with Chinese President Xi Jinping when the two leaders meet next week at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen, China, Trump said, "I don't think so."

"I mean, maybe I'm sure President Xi will ask me about it," Trump said. "I just know Jensen very well. I just think he's a very honorable, honorable man. I mean ... he built a great company, American company, and I think he's a very honorable guy, and I feel very confident that he'll be in good shape."
