#2884. Jensen Huang affair puts Nvidia's future at risk, with no clear successor to the renowned CEO

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's admission of an affair with a purported Chinese spy has raised questions about his future at the company, and what happens if the 63-year-old co-founder, who built the chipmaker into one of the most valuable businesses on the planet, is forced out.

Shares of Nvidia sank more than 4% on Monday, even as the broader market rallied sharply, after Huang acknowledged the affair in a message to staff on Sunday, and said that the board was launching an investigation into whether he was targeted by a foreign intelligence agency. The board said it has "confidence in Jensen," and that it has "no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised."

The New York Times first reported that the Chinese businesswoman was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, according to people briefed on the intelligence circulated in a memo among the Trump administration in the spring. China has called the allegation a smear.

While Nvidia says Huang will continue to lead the company during the probe, pressure is rapidly mounting on the chipmaker, whose technology has powered the first four years of the generative artificial intelligence boom. Leaders from both parties are calling for briefings, and Democrats poised to take control of both houses of Congress are calling for investigations and new legislation "to stop advanced AI chips from fueling China's military," Sen. Elizabeth Warren of Massachusetts wrote in a post on X on Monday.

Huang has outsized influence over Nvidia, and is the sole face of the company with no clear second-in-command. He's also among the loudest champions of AI in the country, is critical to the financing of the broader infrastructure buildout and has a close relationship with President Donald Trump, giving Nvidia a front-row seat when it comes to discussions on AI policy, safety and regulation.

"The issue at Nvidia is that there is no clear successor to Jensen, and we had expected him to stay in this role for at least 10 more years," analysts at Melius Research said in a Monday research note.

Stacy Rasgon, an analyst at Bernstein, told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Monday that, "Jensen is Nvidia. Nvidia is Jensen." He added that Huang "runs the thing pretty much top down," and "sort of pulls all the strings."

Nvidia didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

Nvidia has long maintained a flat, non-hierarchical organizational structure. Huang has deep involvement in the company's day-to-day operations and frequently serves as its spokesperson in front of world leaders and at industry conferences. In addition to being co-founder and CEO, Huang is also Nvidia's president.

In September of 2025, Huang told CNBC's Becky Quick that he had as many as 60 direct reports. Internal records reviewed by CNBC reveal that, as of this week, he had 33 direct reports, including CFO Colette Kress, influential Chief Scientist Bill Dally, senior vice president of software engineering Dwight Diercks, and Ian Buck, best known as the inventor of Nvidia's CUDA software.

However, relative to some of his founder peers in tech like Meta's Mark Zuckerberg and Elon Musk at SpaceX, Huang has minimal actual control. He owns just over 3% of the outstanding shares and is one of 11 members on a board that has no official chair.

And in the risk factors of Nvidia's financial filings, Huang isn't specifically identified as a key person. Rather, the company says, "we are highly dependent on the services of our longstanding executive team."

"Failure to ensure effective succession planning, transfer of knowledge, and smooth transitions involving executives and key employees could hinder our strategic planning, execution, and long-term success," Nvidia says.

Still, Huang's influence is undeniable, so much so that the question of succession is one that's hardly broached among analysts and investors.

Timm Schulze-Melander, an analyst at Rothschild & Co. Redburn, wrote in a Monday note that Huang could be forced to step back from the CEO role if the board's investigation reveals "something materially more serious" than an undisclosed relationship.

But Schulze-Melander said Huang's potential resignation isn't a foregone conclusion of the investigation, pointing to the importance of his leadership at the company.

Nvidia's board "needs to demonstrate independence and protect shareholders without destabilizing a company whose success is unusually dependent on its founder," Schulze-Melander wrote. "Its initial statement suggests it is attempting to achieve both."

Several analysts said the investigation could lead to governance changes at Nvidia, including putting a transition contingency plan in place.

The Melius analysts wrote that Nvidia has a "strong bench" of executive talent across its networking, hyperscale and high-performance computing businesses, along with "technical expertise" stemming from Chief Technology Officer Michael Kagan as well as Dally, the chief scientist.

"While these folks can keep the engine running, Jensen is one of a kind, and his energy will be needed to maintain confidence in the AI trade," the analysts wrote.

— CNBC's Kif Leswing, Kristina Partsinevelos and Michael Bloom contributed to this report.

#2883. Rams star Puka Nacua likely will be placed on injured reserve

The Rams' receiver corps — and the team's aspirations for another Super Bowl title — continue to sustain major hits.

Star Puka Nacua likely will be placed on injured reserve because of a right knee sprain he suffered during the Rams' victory over the Washington Commanders, coach Sean McVay said Monday.

Nacua caught 14 passes for a season-best 150 yards and a touchdown in the 20-13 victory on Sunday in Landover, Md., but he was injured after running a jet sweep on the Rams' final offensive play.

An MRI scan revealed that Nacua sprained his medial collateral and posterior cruciate ligaments. He also has a turf toe injury, McVay said.

Read more: 'I am that closer.' Myles Garrett's three-sack performance shows why Rams traded for him

The Rams' offense was attempting to close out the team's third consecutive victory with a first down that would have enabled them to run out the clock. McVay said he was "kicking myself" about calling on Nacua in that situation.

"I'd be lying to you if I said I haven't questioned the decision-making on that last play over and over in my head of what could have been another option," McVay said during a videoconference with reporters.

Nacua was sidelined two games this season because of a hip injury and was also playing through a calf injury. During the game against the Commanders, he was evaluated for a possible concussion.

If Nacua goes on injured reserve, he will join star receiver Davante Adams, who has been out since suffering a hamstring injury against the Arizona Cardinals in Week 6 at SoFi Stadium.

Players on injured reserve are required to sit out at least four games, so Nacua would miss Sunday's game against the Cardinals and games against the Green Bay Packers, Kansas City Chiefs and San Francisco 49ers before he would be eligible to return against the Dallas Cowboys on Dec. 20.

McVay said Monday he did not have a timeline for Adams' return.

Read more: Puka Nacua shines for Rams in win over Commanders, but his knee injury is a concern

"There are other guys that are very capable of doing some good stuff for us, and we'll look to see those guys step up," McVay said.

Receiver Konata Mumpfield has 11 catches, Tutu Atwell seven, Jordan Whittington two and CJ Daniels none.

McVay has said in the last few weeks that he did not foresee the Rams being active before Tuesday's NFL trade deadline. But Nacua's situation could change that.

"You've got to look at all the avenues to try to be able to solve some of the issues or situations or scenarios that have come up," he said, adding, "I would never rule it out."

Nacua was not the only player injured Sunday who could be sidelined for multiple games.

Left guard Steve Avila left against the Commanders because of a hamstring strain.

NFL teams are limited to having only eight players return from injured reserve. McVay said the Rams were "hoping to avoid" placing Avila on injured reserve but "certainly wouldn't say there's a zero chance."

Cornerback Trent McDuffie, who sat out against the Commanders because of a back injury, is making progress and "hopefully" will be available against the Cardinals, McVay said. ... After the game against the Commanders, McVay said tight end Terrance Ferguson was not activated from injured reserve as planned because of a hamstring injury suffered on Friday. "Probably be another couple weeks for him," McVay said.

#2882. Austin Reaves still had time for jokes about Luka Dončić after big game vs. Pistons

Without Luka Dončić alongside him on Sunday, Austin Reaves showed out with his best performance of the season.

After happily playing second fiddle most of the year, Austin exploded for his first big game this year, scoring 43 points to lead the Lakers to their biggest win of the season against the Pistons. From a shot-making standpoint, it was one of the better games of his career as he knocked down an array of ridiculous floaters, fadeaways and stepbacks.

In many ways, it was Luka-esque. And even Austin admitted as much. To cap off a spurt midway through the third quarter, Austin beat the shot clock with a 3-pointer that banked in.

No, he didn't do it on purpose. No, it wasn't a good shot. And yes, it was some "bulls—" Luka would shoot, too.

"Obviously Luka is an incredible player and I think sometimes, when they're playing together, AR kind of has to take a secondary role kind of," Walker Kessler said. "But, as you can see now, he's very capable. He's a heck of a playmaker, a heck of a scorer and a competitor. He played great."

It was an eventful night for Austin overall. During the first half, he got into a face-to-face chat with a fan after being knocked to the floor in front of them. At the time, it was unclear if it was a friendly conversation or one of warning for Austin.

Late in the game, Austin effectively sealed the win with a dagger 3-pointer with just under a minute remaining. He took some of the mystery out of the type of conversation he had with the fan by turning and pointing at him while waving goodbye.

But if you were looking for some kind of clarity on the specifics of the conversation and what was said, Austin, coyly, wasn't playing ball postgame.

What happens on the court stays on the court, it seems. Unless you're Yaxel Lendeborg.

#2880. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2878. Jaguars acquire Jessie Bates III from Falcons for 2 picks, also get Davon Godchaux, Jordan Davis in trio of defensive trades

The Atlanta Falcons have apparently decided to play for the future, while the Jacksonville Jaguars are going all-in.

A day after falling to the Cincinnati Bengals 34-17 in Madrid, the Falcons reportedly traded safety Jessie Bates to the Jaguars for two draft picks, according to ESPN. Jacksonville will give up a 2027 second-round pick and a 2028 fifth-round pick in exchange for Bates.

However, that was far from the only move the Jaguars made on Monday. Shortly, the team reportedly acquired New Orleans Saints defensive tackle Davon Godchaux in a deal, according to The Ringer. The Jaguars gave up a sixth-round pick in exchange for Godchaux.

And hours after that, Jacksonville picked up another blockbuster trade, landing DT Jordan Davis while sending multiple picks to the Philadelphia Eagles.

Bates has a pretty lengthy history of success in the NFL. Though Bates has made just one Pro Bowl in his career, he's been selected to three All-Pro second teams. His most recent selection came last season, when he made 98 tackles for the Falcons.

Bates, 29, is having another excellent start in 2026. Through nine games, he's registered 60 tackles and three forced fumbles. He'll take those skills to an already-dominant Jaguars defense, which is allowing the second-fewest points per game.

It cost Jacksonville a heavy price, especially considering Bates is in the final year of his contract. Barring an extension, he'll be a free agent at the end of the 2026 season.

While other AFC teams have been impressive, the Jaguars might have the best all-around team in the league. The addition of Bates, along with Godchaux and Davis, should only help bolster that claim.

For the Falcons, the move signals the team will likely play for 2027. After Sunday's loss to the Bengals, the Falcons dropped to 3-6 on the season.

With the playoffs unlikely, the Falcons will start selling off any player who won't play a significant role with the team moving forward. Bates was the first — and could be the biggest — name traded as the team shifts its focus to the future.

#2876. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2872. ’Hoos in the NBA: Early-season report card

The Virginia Cavaliers men's basketball program is enjoying an excellent start to the season. The current 'Hoos earned a statement win over No. 12 Arkansas in Washington, D.C., on Sunday afternoon to improve to 3-0, while a handful of former Virginia stars are enjoying strong starts to their seasons at the next level.

With most NBA teams reaching the 10-game checkpoint over the weekend, here's how the former faces of UVA basketball have been faring in The Association so far.

There's no better place to start than with Ty Jerome, who has become one of the NBA's feel-good stories early in the season. While Virginia fans have long known what Jerome is capable of, the national basketball audience is finally getting its formal introduction to the 6-foot-5 assassin from New Rochelle, New York.

After years of dealing with injury while intermittently posting stretches of highly efficient and majorly underappreciated play, Jerome has erupted in the early going for Memphis, averaging 26.6 points and 5.3 assists per game while piloting the Grizzlies to an unexpected 6-3 start.

The former Virginia star is playing the best basketball of his pro career, and Memphis is reaping the rewards. Jerome has already racked up two 40+ point performances this season plus a highlight reel's worth of clutch shots. Averaging a league-best 14 points per game on drives, Jerome's signature floater has become one of the league's deadliest weapons.

Coming off back-to-back seasons averaging north of 20 points per game, Trey Murphy III is back to knocking down threes and throwing down eye-popping dunks for the Pelicans.

Though his raw numbers are down slightly this season (15.6 points, 4.8 rebounds per game), his three-point efficiency (37.7%) remains sky-high.

The ever-efficient Huff went off for a season-high 22 points (8-for-14 FG) and eight rebounds last Monday night against the Atlanta Hawks.

The sharp-shooting Sam Hauser continues to be one of the league's most valuable three-point specialists for the Boston Celtics. Playing 21 minutes per game (mostly off the bench, though he did start one game for Boston last week against the Milwaukee Bucks), Hauser is averaging 7.7 points per game on 40.4% shooting from the outside.

A 2024 first-round pick by Phoenix, Ryan Dunn's third professional season is off to an efficient start despite a somewhat limited role in the Suns' rotation.

The 6-foot-7 defensive ace is averaging 5.6 points, 3.6 rebounds, 1.4 stocks per game while playing 16 minutes per game for the 3-5 Suns and is coming off his best week of the season, posting a season-high 11 points in Thursday night's win against the Orlando Magic and 8 points in a weekend victory over the Houston Rockets.

With Dunn, the question is always going to be his three-point shooting. And so far this season, he's knocked down 7-of-13 attempts from distance.

The former ACC All-Defensive Team selection at Virginia in 2023-24 has yet to shoot better than 33.1% from deep in a season, but if he can keep his early-season stroke going, 'Hoos fans are well aware of the eye-popping athleticism that made Dunn such an intriguing pro prospect coming out of Charlottesville.

Beekman, now a third-year pro, signed a two-way contract with the Washington Wizards this past offseason after a year in the G-League in 2025-26. Unfortunately, Beekman is currently dealing with an acromioclavicular joint injury that will keep him sidelined for at least three weeks.

Meanwhile, the other former 'Hoo in the Nation's Capital, Anthony Gill, is the longest-tenured player on the Washington Wizards' roster. Gill has become the ultimate locker-room presence and player-coach for a franchise trying to transition from a rebuild to contention in the Eastern Conference. The seventh-year NBA vet has appeared in just two games this season, but his wisdom and steadying presence are immeasurable for the young Wizards.

#2869. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2868. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2867. Doc Rivers calls Sixers NBA’s best team

The mere mention of Doc Rivers' name is bound to get some (maybe most) Sixers fans feeling some kind of way. Be that as it may, Rivers was on Bill Simmons' podcast last Friday, throwing flowers in the direction of the team he coached from 2020-23.

The episode dropped before the Sixers split a pair of road games over the weekend, leaving them with a league-best 9-2 record. It is their fastest start since the Allen Iverson-led club won its first 10 games in 2000-01, en route to a Finals appearance.

"Right now," Rivers said, "they're the best team in the NBA. They're gonna be hard to beat."

The 65-year-old Rivers, whose 27-year run as a head coach appears to be at an end after he spent the last three seasons as the Milwaukee Bucks' boss, went on to say that Jaylen Brown is among the three best small forwards in the league, that Joel Embiid is no worse than the fourth-best center and that Tyrese Maxey is among the top five point guards.

"And LeBron (James), whatever he is, top five," Rivers said. "A four (i.e., power forward) right now."

"Top five defender at the 2-spot right now," Rivers said.

"I still think that, and I've said this before, anybody can go out and get the stars," he said. "It's the other guys. And I think that's where they hit the home run. … (Anfernee) Simons, (Kentavious) Caldwell-Pope, Dean Wade."

Pope, 33, is in his 14th season. He is, Rivers said, "perfect for this team, because he's winning. He's playing a role to win. Guys like Caldwell-Pope, late in their career, they can't play on a bad team. It just doesn't fit. They're trying to play right. Now he's in a perfect role."

And to the veteran coach Simons "looks better," even though his shooting has been erratic.

"He looks like this year, he's accepted his role even more," Rivers said.

Rivers reserved his greatest praise for LeBron and Maxey.

"I like LeBron's role right now," he said. "Love it."

Later, he added, "LeBron is who I would have loved him to have been early in his career (i.e., a playmaker), even though he had to score. He's the closest to Magic Johnson to ever have played, as far as being big and able to facilitate. LeBron's vision is still underrated – like, he's not a good passer; he's a great passer, and he sees it early."

James' scoring average (15.6) is far and away the lowest of his 24-year career, but he is averaging around seven assists, as well as seven rebounds.

"It's the happiest I've seen him in a long time," Rivers said. "I don't think he was ever that happy in LA (while playing for the Lakers the last eight seasons) – like this happiness. I'm not saying he wasn't happy in LA, but this is true happiness."

Maxey, meanwhile, seems content no matter the circumstances. Rivers saw that for himself when he coached him in Philadelphia, and continues to regard the young point guard as one of the most upbeat players he's ever been around.

"Maxey's one of these guys that can fit on any team," he said. "I was telling Grant Hill (the managing director of USA Basketball), if he's not on the Olympic team, you're crazy, because he's just going to do good. Even if he's not playing, he smiles. He's happy. He's competitive. And he's a phenomenal basketball player."

Rivers' wide-ranging conversation with Simmons also included a mention of former Sixer Matisse Thybulle, who has settled into a rotational role with the Lakers, his third team in eight NBA seasons.

"I'm happy for him," Rivers said. "I didn't think he loved the game enough. … He kind of was this flower child. Honestly right now, watching him play, it's like he kind of realized, 'I was almost out of the league.' … He's a smart kid, and he kind of realized, 'You know what, I've got to get cooking here.'"

Rivers called Thybulle "a hell of a defender" while adding that he "gambles too much." He also invoked the memory of the December 2021 game between the Sixers and Warriors. Entering play Stephen Curry needed 10 three-pointers to break Ray Allen's career record, but Thybulle limited him to three triples in 14 attempts, and became the first player to block two of Curry's threes in a single game. In all Curry managed 18 points on 6-for-20 shooting in the 102-93 Sixers victory.

Rivers said on the Simmons pod that while he and Curry are family – Stephen's brother Seth, another ex-Sixer, is married to Rivers' daughter Callie – he had made it clear in family discussions he did not want to see Steph break the record that night.

"It was all on Matisse," Doc said. "He guarded the hell out of him. Because Steph takes some shots, and here comes Matisse from behind, with those long arms. He recovers. So yeah, he can really defend."

#2866. Video: ‘UFC 334 Countdown’ full episode

Josh Hokit challenges Ciryl Gane for the heavyweight title, and Kayla Harrison and Amanda Nunes have one of the most highly anticipated women's MMA fights of all time at UFC 334 this weekend in Madison Square Garden.

In the main event on Saturday, Ciryl Gane puts his new undisputed heavyweight title on the line against one of the fastest-rising and most controversial figures in MMA, Josh Hokit. Gane won an interim title at UFC White House by stopping Alex Pereira (though with some controversy), and was elevated to full champion in September after Tom Aspinall vacated his title due to lingering injury issues. Hokit, meanwhile, made his UFC debut just a year ago, but quickly amassed four wins and surged up the heavyweight rankings through a combination of stellar performances and controversial mic work.

The stakes are arguably higher in the co-main event, as women's MMA GOAT Amanda Nunes finally makes her return to the cage from retirement, looking to reclaim the bantamweight title she vacated by challenging former teammate and two-time Olympic gold medalist Kayla Harrison. This fight has been years in the making and was originally booked for early this year before an injury sidelined Harrison, but now the two all-time greats finally meet to settle things.

#2865. Another trade! Jaguars land their defensive game-changer in Jordan Davis

If there was any lingering doubt about the Jacksonville Jaguars' ambitions this season, James Gladstone has just put it to bed.

The Jaguars have agreed a blockbuster trade with the Philadelphia Eagles for defensive tackle Jordan Davis, sending a 2027 first-round pick and a 2029 second-round pick to Philadelphia in exchange for the former first-round selection.

It's the third defensive addition Jacksonville has made in a remarkable day of activity, following the acquisitions of Davon Godchaux from New Orleans and Jessie Bates III from Atlanta.

But this is a different level of investment. Jacksonville hasn't simply dipped into its draft capital to patch up a few holes. It has surrendered two premium selections to acquire a 26-year-old defensive lineman who could become a cornerstone of its defense for years to come.

The Jaguars aren't just buying to secure a place in the playoffs. They're buying for a Super Bowl.

There aren't many players in the NFL who can physically dominate the interior of an offensive line quite like Jordan Davis.

Standing at 6-foot-6 and weighing 336 pounds, Davis is an imposing presence who brings an entirely different dimension to Jacksonville's defensive front.

Selected 13th overall in the 2022 NFL Draft, Davis arrived in Philadelphia with enormous expectations. His development has seen him become an important component of one of the league's most formidable defensive fronts, earning a Super Bowl ring along the way.

His 2025 campaign was arguably his best yet. Davis recorded career highs of 72 tackles, 4.5 sacks and nine tackles for loss, demonstrating that he could offer more than simply occupying blockers and clogging running lanes.

He's registered 31 tackles, 1.5 sacks and two tackles for loss through nine starts this season. Those numbers don't necessarily jump off the page, but Davis' value extends beyond the traditional box score. His ability to occupy multiple blockers, control the line of scrimmage and create opportunities for the players around him makes him an incredibly valuable defensive asset.

For defensive coordinator Anthony Campanile, adding a player of Davis' physical profile could be transformational. The Jaguars have already lost Davon Hamilton for the season, creating an obvious need in the middle of their defensive line. Godchaux provides an experienced replacement, but Davis offers something considerably more exciting: a potential long-term answer at the position.

And there's a further attraction. Davis has spent his entire professional career playing in Philadelphia's defensive system under Vic Fangio, giving Jacksonville another player with experience in a defensive environment that shares some philosophical similarities with Campanile's approach.

Let's not pretend this is a cheap acquisition.

A first-round pick in 2027 and a second-round selection in 2029 is an enormous amount of draft capital to surrender for one defensive player. Especially when you consider that Chicago recently paid two first-round picks to acquire Tennessee Titans defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons.

But context matters. Jacksonville is 6-2, sitting atop the AFC South and playing some outstanding football. Its offense is among the league's best, while Campanile has developed a defense capable of competing with anybody.

The opportunity is there. The Jaguars have recognised it, and they're acting accordingly.

There's also a longer-term consideration. Davis is only 26 and was signed to a three-year, $78 million contract extension by Philadelphia in March. This isn't a rental player brought in for a desperate postseason push. Jacksonville is acquiring a player who can help them now and remain an important part of the defensive front beyond this season.

The first-round pick could ultimately be a relatively late selection if the Jaguars continue on their current trajectory. That doesn't make it insignificant, but it does change the calculation. At some point, teams have to stop treating draft picks as lottery tickets and start using them to improve a roster capable of winning a championship.

This is a question that needs answering. Whenever a blue-chip player gets moved from one roster to another, there has to be a reason, right?

The Eagles have invested heavily in Davis, handing him a three-year, $78 million extension earlier this year. He's also been an important leader in their locker room, making his departure all the more surprising.

However, Philadelphia's season has taken a significant turn for the worse. The Eagles have been struggling, and moving a player of Davis' value for two premium draft selections gives Howie Roseman an opportunity to reshape the roster.

The return is substantial. Philadelphia now has an additional first-round pick in 2027 and another second-round selection in 2029, giving Roseman even more ammunition to replenish a roster that has some significant questions to answer.

There is also the situation surrounding Moro Ojomo. Philadelphia had reportedly been reluctant to move him, and Davis' departure potentially creates a clearer path towards retaining the younger defensive tackle.

It's a significant financial and football decision, particularly after extending Davis just eight months ago. But Roseman has built his reputation on knowing when to sell, and he's extracted a substantial return here.

The Eagles may ultimately be comfortable with the decision. Jacksonville certainly will be hoping they regret it.

There's always a danger in handing out top grades for blockbuster trades. Draft capital matters, and surrendering a first and second-round pick is a considerable commitment. But the Jaguars have a genuine opportunity to do something special this season.

They've lost important defensive starters to injury, identified the positions that need strengthening and gone out to acquire established NFL talent. Godchaux brings experience. Bates brings leadership and playmaking ability. Davis brings size, power, youth and the potential to anchor the defensive interior for the foreseeable future.

Most importantly, these additions complement a roster that was already performing at an exceptionally high level. James Gladstone and Liam Coen clearly believe this team is ready to compete for the biggest prize in football. Rather than sitting back and hoping their existing roster is good enough, they've invested heavily in making sure it is.

This is what going ALL IN looks like. The Jaguars have made their intentions abundantly clear, and the rest of the league have just been put on notice.

#2864. Austin Reaves keeps it real about miraculous shot: ‘B——- Luka would shoot’

DETROIT — Austin Reaves couldn't pretend it was a good shot.

Even with the Lakers star guard having the greenest of green lights after going on a personal 7-0 run as the hosts attempted to come back from down 26 points, Reaves kept it real about what followed.

Austin Reaves hit a variety of big shots in the Lakers' win over the Pistons. AP Photo/Duane Burleson

After John Collins nudged the ball out of his hands after running a pick and roll with Walker Kessler, Reaves lost possession of the ball in his left hand with his momentum taking him toward the sideline opposite of the Pistons' bench.

But he was able to get the ball back to the hottest hand on the court throughout Sunday night, regaining possession and taking a single dribble before firing up a 27-footer with 11 seconds still on the shot clock.

Austin Reaves powered the Lakers' offense with Luka Doncic sitting out against the Pistons. Getty Images

"It was a terrible shot," Reaves admitted postgame. "Absolutely."

It didn't matter, with the ball bouncing off the top of the backboard before falling through the net to give the Lakers a 78-61 lead and putting Reaves at 32 points after scoring 10 points in just over two minutes of game time for his first 30-point game of the season.

The 6-foot-5 Oklahoma product didn't even try to act like he called bank, with the shot reminding him of the kind of miraculous looks his backcourt co-star regularly attempts.

"Oh, it was 100% lucky," he said. "Honestly, when I stepped back and shot it, I was like, 'this is some b——- Luka [Dončić] would shoot.' And I didn't try to bank it in. It went in, it looks the same in the stat sheet."

Austin Reaves secured his first 40-point game of the season. NBAE via Getty Images

The 3-pointer was just one of the many wild shots Reaves made en route to scoring a season-high 43 points in the Lakers' 121-116 win over the Pistons at Little Caesars Arena.

He also hit a fadeaway from behind the backboard midway through the first quarter for his seventh and eighth points of the night which gave the Lakers an early 18-7 lead.

"Lucky shot," Reaves said. "I was a lucky player [Sunday night]."

And with Dončić, the league's leading scorer with an average of 40.1 points per game, watching from the sidelines against the Pistons because of back tightness, the Lakers greatly benefitted from Reaves' luck.

#2863. What Coco Gauff was overheard saying to herself during win over Jessica Pegula which explains why she’s so good

Coco Gauff is seeking to win a second WTA Finals title in Indian Wells this week.

The American took the first step towards accomplishing her goal after beating Jessica Pegula on Sunday evening.

In what was a back and forth affair, Gauff eventually triumphed 6-4, 6-7, 6-2 in California.

Following Sunday's contest, Andrea Petkovic and Brad Gilbert were full of praise for the 22-year-old American star.

"I had a great seat next to Garbine Muguruza, tournament director here, and I saw one thing with Coco, and I was like, 'Man, that's why she's so good,' former world number nine Andrea Petkovic said on the Big T Podcast.

"So she came back to 5-5, [then] 6-5, and she was basically returning to break Jess's serve that she had just broken twice and back to back to win the match, so very important game, right?

"[At] 6-5 in the second set, Jess hits an okay, medium good wide first serve, and Coco misses a forehand return, and she turned around and I could read her lips, and she kind of clapped and said to herself, 'That was a good serve' about Jess's serve.

"And that was such an insight into her mind because every other player that I know, me including, would have reacted like, 'How can I miss this return?' And especially when you know they're going to serve it there."

"And you know what is special about Coco too is how resilient she was," Brad Gilbert, the former coach of Andre Agassi, Andy Murray and Coco Gauff, added. "What about that hold at 4-4 in the first set? 0-40 and then you knew she was going to break the next game.

"But I thought she did a great job of just battling in that second set, 5-1 down to even put herself in that position to close out the second set, and I do think fitness will play a factor in this tournament.

"It is nice that you know the ladies that a lot of them play six, seven, eight weeks in Asia, all outdoors on hardcore. So we're here on the same surface, but it was almost 90 degrees today.

2022 – lost to Iga Swiatek, Caroline Garcia and Daria Kasatkina in the group stage.

2023 – defeated Ons Jabeur and Marketa Vondrousova in the group stage before losing to Jessica Pegula in the semi-finals

2024 – won her first WTA Finals crown, beating Zheng Qinwen 3-6, 6-4, 7-6 in the final

2025 – exited in the group stage after losing to Aryna Sabalenka and Jessica Pegula

2026 – won her first match against Jessica Pegula, 6-4, 6-7, 6-2

"If you haven't played a tournament in three weeks, you can't simulate match play. So all of a sudden, it's like it can hit you, and I I definitely think Jess's legs were a little bit weary in the end of that."

Gauff will look to secure her place in the semi-finals when she competes against Marta Kostyuk and Linda Noskova later this week.

The two-time Grand Slam champion has a 2-1 record against Noskova and a 4-2 record against Kostyuk.

Gauff is set to play Kostyuk on Tuesday, following the conclusion of Pegula's match against Noskova.

Gauff was made to work hard by Pegula on Sunday, before the the 22-year-old demonstrated once again why she is one of the most dogged players on the WTA Tour.

As per OptaAce, Gauff has now tied Pegula for the most three-set victories this year. Both Gauff and Pegula have won 19 three-set matches throughout 2026.

Gauff and Pegula could play each other again before the end of 2026. However, they would both need to reach the final of the WTA Finals to do so.

Realistically, after losing on Sunday, Pegula will need to defeat Marta Kostyuk on Tuesday to stand any chance of advancing to the semi-finals in California.

#2862. Millions of dollars poured into prediction markets on election night, trading on Democratic control of Congress and Texas governor's race

More than 87% of all election markets across Kalshi and Polymarket gave the right candidate at least a 90% chance of winning their race 24 hours before election night, a CNBC analysis found.

Election night showed how traders reacted to real-time midterm results, as trading activity surged and market prices shifted to Democrats after results from Indiana began rolling in before 7 p.m. on Nov. 3. Many midterm races were uncompetitive, which boosted the overall hit rate on prediction markets.

"I'd say prediction markets did pretty well," said Koleman Strumpf, a professor of economics at Wake Forest University. "But they didn't outperform the best," he added, referring to traditional forecast polls that tracked results that night.

Odds and gambling platforms do not use methodologies used by traditional political polling, and therefore are not substitutes for political polls.

Prediction markets saw big trades on election night.

After 8 p.m. on Nov. 3, at least $1 million was traded on a Kalshi market that incumbent Gov. Greg Abbott would be reelected. Abbott eventually lost to Democrat Gina Hinojosa by more than 700,000 votes, according to the Associated Press. A little after 11 p.m. on election night, more than $1.4 million was traded on Kalshi that Republicans would not secure control of the House of Representatives.

Following the midterms, both Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan and Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour were on the social media platform X crediting prediction markets for correctly predicting races. Nate Silver, a political forecaster and founder of the Silver Bulletin newsletter, disputed the claim, arguing forecast models influenced prediction market movement. Silver joined Polymarket as an advisor in 2024.

"Prediction market probabilities strongly converged toward [our models] in individual Senate and gubernatorial races," Silver wrote on Silver Bulletin. "I was tracking this stuff closely, precisely because I envisioned that we might wind up in this sort of debate where prediction markets are low-key stealing valor from the models."

Prediction market volume on midterm election-related contracts across Kalshi and Polymarket more than tripled from the last week of October into the midterms week, according to a CNBC analysis of data published on Predictions.com. The data did not include combo markets, which bundle multiple contracts and pay out only if every leg resolves in the trader's favor.

Still, volume on midterm contracts pales in comparison with sports volume, which remains larger than all other categories.

Kalshi exceeded $3 billion in total midterm contract volume, according to the platform's website, which also includes volume for contracts on mayoral races. Last week, Polymarket's two exchanges reported nearly $700 million in volume for midterm contracts.

While prediction markets broadly anticipated Democratic control of Congress, the blue tsunami is expected to bite the industry in other ways.

Three days after the midterms, Sen. Adam Schiff, D-Calif, criticized the federal regulator responsible for the platforms, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, for allowing sports contracts.

"Sports prediction markets are sports bets. By effectively requiring prediction markets to offer contracts in all 50 states, even on Tribal lands, the CFTC is once again violating state law and Tribal sovereignty," Schiff wrote on X.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2861. A massive trade in Nvidia just went off – here are the details

The biggest options trade on Nvidia stock Monday seems to think the controversy swirling around the chip leader and its embattled CEO is creating a buying opportunity.In a trade about an hour after the opening bell that involved more than $100 million in options premium, someone exchanged an existing call spread for a more bullish position that captures the next five months of trading in Nvidia.The core position was the purchase of 52,000 195-strike calls expiring Feb. 19 worth about $57 million – contracts the trader opened for a new long position while simultaneously selling the same number of 245-strike calls for the same expiry, lowering the cost of the trade by $10 million. The net position, a bullish call spread, needs Nvidia to trade above around $206 by expiry, or about 15% higher, to be profitable. If Nvidia stock reaches the short, 245-strike call, it would represent a nearly 40% rally and return the stock to its all-time high of $243, that was reached on October 6th.The trade was likely part of a "roll" down from an existing 210/255-strike call spread worth about $42 million that was closed out, according to SpotGamma analysis.

"The person got more aggressive rolling this spread down to lower strikes to get more upside exposure by getting higher delta," SpotGamma founder Brent Kochuba said by phone. "They're trading the call spread here because the vol is expensive going into earnings. It's a significant increase in upside delta exposure."Delta is options parlance for describing how quickly an options price will react to a change in the underlying stock price. The trader's updated position, taken at lower strikes, will make money faster if Nvidia rallies. In other words, it's a higher conviction trade that the chipmaker's stock will soon rebound. The 195-strike calls they bought for $57 million was the single biggest options trade on the tape Monday, according to SpotGamma. Of the top 20 options trades by dollar-amount, 12 were Nvidia, the data show.Flows around the stock generally leaned bullish by midday, with 57% of options volume tied to calls and more than $660 million of call premium likely initiated by call-buyers, SpotGamma data show. The most popular single contract by volume was the 180-strike call expiring Monday, that needs Nvidia to recover 1.2% by the bell to break even.

#2860. NFL trade deadline: The Jaguars are all-in; should the 49ers follow suit?

The San Francisco 49ers have the best record in the NFL right now, with the Minnesota Vikings playing tonight on Monday Night Football. Trade rumors are swirling, and the team suffered a blow with the loss of defensive tackle Gracen Halton. The trade deadline is tomorrow, and there is plenty of time left to add to the roster.

The Jacksonville Jaguars have sent a message to the AFC and the NFL with the acquisitions of safety Jessie Bates and defensive tackle Jordan Davis. It feels like just about any year you can say the NFL is "wide open," but this season seems to be more open than seasons past.

Kyle Shanahan and John Lynch haven't indicated they are all-in or completely out in public statements. In my opinion, I'd bet the 49ers are working the phones right now. Evaluating the in-house talent and the incoming reinforcements is part of the equation for the 49ers. We do know the 49ers targeted Jauan Jennings in a trade with Minnesota, but that move felt like a depth improvement, given his familiarity with the offense.

Should the 49ers take a page from the Jaguars' playbook? Now, look, teams shouldn't be reactionary to other teams' moves or thought processes. It is possible for the 49ers to be fine with what they have. There is the possibility that the 49ers, who have run the ball very well in 2026, will shift their identity in January to a running team that controls the clock and wants their defense to eliminate offenses.

You don't suspect you will be in shootouts in the playoffs, and of course anything is possible. On the flip side, adding firepower is never a bad idea, provided the value of the return and the investment is reasonable. Do the 49ers have confidence in the returns of Mike Evans, Deebo Samuel, and De'Zhaun Stribling, potentially with Demarcus Robinson rounding into shape?

The pass rush hasn't flashed as much as you would like, but do the 49ers add a familiar face off the street, Leonard Floyd, anyone? The 49ers have taken swings in the past. Will the 49ers stand pat or bet on the return of CJ West to bolster the defensive tackle depth?

Should the 49ers swing on a wide receiver or hope that Jordan Watkins' development will continue? Calling the Jets about Will McDonald IV isn't the worst idea. Sound off in the comments: should the 49ers follow Jacksonville's trade process?

#2859. Bright Side Wonders, Vol 7: The Suns have a win streak

In the third week of the NBA season, the Phoenix Suns had four games, the same number they played in the first two weeks combined. Losing the first two and winning the last two, the team has established some momentum after a rough 1-5 start to the year, but there's a lot they need to fix if they want to return to the playoffs.

Alas, here are the questions we want your answers on for this week's edition of Bright Side Wonders:

How do you feel about the Durant trade now that the Suns have finally beaten him?

After starting the year 1-5, the Phoenix Suns made a change to the starting lineup. And no, Collin Gillespie is still a reserve. Oso Ighodaro has replaced Khaman Maluach at the starting center spot, and the team is 2-0 since. The two's playing time hasn't altered much since the move, but who they're playing with has, and the Suns' starting lineup's net rating goes up by 16 points when Ighodaro is the starter, according to HoopsJunkie.

Ighodaro isn't the rebounder or the physical presence that Maluach is, but he's more experienced, and adds another playmaker to a starting lineup that lacks them. Additionally, the fact that he doesn't need to have the ball to make an impact helps a starting five full of ball-dominant scorers.

Albeit a small sample size, how are you feeling about the lineup change?

When the Suns traded for Miles Bridges, it was known that someone in the starting lineup was going to have to make a shot attempt sacrifice, but it was hard to believe it'd be Dillon Brooks after his career year and it's been him, and the Suns aren't benefiting from it.

While he's shooting career-highs from the field and three, the team is 14 points worse on offense when he's on the floor. Even though he makes up for it by being such a major impact on defense, one of the reasons the Suns surprised many last season was because of his offensive aggressiveness, and with him taking nearly five less shots per game than he did a season ago, they're struggling?

What impact would a more aggressive Brooks make on the team's offense?

#2857. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2855. Harry Kane reflects on emotional Ballon d’Or triumph and reveals secret behind his success

The Bayern Munich striker admitted he had never experienced such pressure before, despite the numerous high-profile matches he has played throughout his career, as he reflected on the moment his name was announced as the winner.

“I went through every possible emotion. I was really nervous. I had never felt that kind of pressure before, even after all the football matches I’ve played, because this time, the outcome wasn’t really in my hands,” Kane explained in an interview with Téléfoot.

“When I walked onto the stage, I felt an incredible sense of relief. My heart rate was really high. I was almost frozen when I heard my name. Everything around me just stopped, like in a movie. It was a very nice feeling.”

Kane also discussed the message he shared following his triumph, writing: ‘Work hard in silence. Let success be your noise.’

The England captain revealed that the quote has resonated with him for several years, reflecting the mentality that has helped him reach the highest level of the game.

“It’s something I’ve come across many years ago, when I was younger. I think it’s just been something that suits me as a person,” he said.

The 33-year-old highlighted the importance of the work that supporters rarely get to see, pointing towards additional treatment, ice baths and finishing sessions after training as examples of the dedication that has helped him continually improve.

“It’s almost what I enjoy the most sometimes. When I look back through my journey, when things looked not so good, I think it’s what makes the journey so special,” Kane added.

Having initially admitted that winning football’s most prestigious individual award was beyond his expectations, Kane explained how his ambitions gradually developed as his career progressed.

“I simply wanted to become a good footballer, and then I gradually improved over time. As my career progressed, I became more interested in the Ballon d’Or. I started thinking, ‘Why not?’ But winning this award is beyond anything I could have imagined.”

Kane also credited his move to Munich with helping him reach another level, insisting that he is currently playing the best football of his career.

“Since arriving in Munich, I’ve taken another step forward. Today, I’m in the best form of my life. I hope to give my very best until the end,” he concluded.

With his individual achievements now matching his outstanding performances on the pitch, Kane’s comments underline the mentality and dedication that have helped him reach the pinnacle of world football.

#2854. Basketball puberty? The Raptors’ growing pains and breakouts through the road trip

Teenage life is complicated, and while we're in the 2020s, it seems that the Toronto Raptors are going through an awkward phase right now. With a big brother (Kawhi) out of the house for college (injury treatment), they're having to find themselves in a big and scary high school (Eastern Conference) as they're trying to develop an identity for themselves in an unforgiving world (the National Basketball Association).

More threes, and a Kawhi Leonard and Scottie Barnes centred offence, would have both been logical progressions from last year's Raptors' squad. But, Leonard out and some streaky shooting has meant that the Raptors are looking like quite a different beast entirely. Tonight, the Raptors are matching up with the Grizzlies, a familiar foe from the Vancouver days, with the possibility to have three starters out, due to Leonard's injury management and some issues exhibited from Scottie Barnes and Immanuel Quickley.

Barnes and Quickley are both focal parts of the Raptors' offence, but didn't play in the team's last game, a victory against the Milwaukee Bucks. Across the four game road trip, the Raps went two and two, picking up wins against two really, really bad teams (Clippers and Bucks) and losing to a great team and a fine team (Lakers and Nuggets). Not super heartening, but again, the first major road trip, and all of those games were played with at least one starter down.

The road trip continued to showcase Scottie Barnes' development into a type of player that we only saw glimpses of during the last playoffs. Barnes, this season, has transformed himself into a rebounding, midrange shooting, volume scorer as opposed to the pass-first point forward that he had developed a reputation as.

As of time of writing, Scottie is second in the league for midranges attempted, and third in the league for midranges made. This is, quite frankly, bananas, considering that Barnes was never known to be a primary scorer; it seems that one summer with Kawhi Leonard has rubbed off a lot on the younger player. Barnes has never been more fun to watch, while still maintaining his responsibilities on the defensive end, averaging 2.3 steals on the season so far.

Barnes has shown that he's able to do something that superstars are often called upon to do: prop up a mediocre team with great scoring, taking over like he did during the win against the Clippers. And indeed, the Raptors have thus far been unfortunately mediocre.

"We've been very underwhelming to start the season," said Jamal Shead after the Raptors' win against the Bucks last week. "We have so much more to put out, and I think today was a start," he continued.

Consistency is a big part of that. Kristian Cuaresma pointed to the loss to the Denver Nuggets being a tale of four quarters: the Raptors take 12 threes in the first quarter, and make six. Immanuel Quickley gets 10 points and six times in 12 minutes. But then, the Raptors shoot 6-22 from three for the rest of the game, and Immanuel Quickley ends the game, scoring just three more points, and getting one more assist.

In the game against the Los Angeles Clippers, Barnes finished with 22, but only had seven before the fourth quarter.

And, in the game against the Bucks, Jakob Poeltl plays 31 points and gets a 19 point, 11 rebound double-double. Yes, the Bucks are abysmal, but when Poeltl does that in one game, but has a rough stretch like he does at the beginning of the season, it's difficult to know what version of the big man the Raptors are getting on a night-to-night basis, at this juncture.

Understanding team identity is important, and clearly, the idea of Scottie Barnes as a point forward supporting Kawhi Leonard, and the Raptors developing a major three point shooting offence isn't developing quite as anyone thought it might. And ultimately, that's okay. But this lack of dependable production (and availability) from just about everyone on the Raptors means that every game is shaping up to be a very different kind of contest, where weapons end up being used in majorly different ways.

If Darko Rajaković understands, and is able to harness his players' varying levels of production going forward, there won't be an issue. But, the alternative, a confusing and inconsistent team, would be devastating after making such a big swing for Kawhi Leonard.

Jamal Shead might have been speaking for his team with his statement: the Raptors can be doing a lot more, and perhaps will be able to turn the corner now into a more polished version of what they bring to the hardwood. But finally cementing the identity of this iteration of the Raptors is a project that seems far from finished. Growing up takes a lot of time, and a lot of pain for everyone involved, and Toronto seems to be no exception.
